## Getting started

### What is Traversence?
Traversence is a regional travel guide and local directory in one place. **Let's Explore** tells the story of a place and the journeys people take there. **Get Local** is the directory of businesses, services and places. You can use both without an account.

### What's on the home page?
The search comes first, in two parts: **What are you looking for?** (type what you're after; × clears it) and **Let's get local** (where: tap it and the search's window opens at the place picker, to pick a town, ZIP or area and **Distance**, how far around it to look; or tap the target to use your location. It then shows the place you're searching). Searching opens its own window over the page, with its own box for your words, the place you're searching (tap it and the place and Distance open right there in the same window, in place of the results; pick a place, or tap it again or × to go back) and the results, so nothing behind it gets in the way. On a phone it fills the screen. Pick a result, or click outside the window, tap ×, or press Esc to close it. Then:
- **Four ways in**, one for each main section: **Let's Explore** (places, trails, history), **Get Local** (businesses and services), **Connect** (groups) and the **Marketplace**.
- **Community Groups** on Connect. Next to the groups already running are a few a place usually needs but doesn't have yet (your town's neighbors, the outdoors, local history, families, volunteers, business owners), each with **Start this group**.
- **Events** near you, from the places' own calendars. Until there are enough, a few cards marked **Sample** show how events will look. Buying tickets is coming.
- **What's new**, newest first, with five tabs: **Events**, **Deals**, **Gallery** (public photo albums), **Journey** (journeys and guides) and **Business** (businesses run by their owners). Tap a tab to see only that kind, and again to see everything. Deals from businesses are coming.
- **Have a GO Code?**: type the code from a sign or flyer to open its page.

Nothing on the home page is paid placement: it's newest first.

### How do I contact Traversence?
Email **support@traversence.com**. It's at the foot of every page, and in the emails we send you (or just reply to one of them: replies come to us too). Ask anything: your account, a listing, a GO Code order, or your privacy and data.

### Where did the big logo and icons at the top go?
The top bar starts collapsed, so the page gets the whole screen: no bar, just the compass in the top-left corner and the menu (☰) hanging from the top-right corner, with search between them on pages that have it (on the home page the search sits in the page itself, under the title). Signed in, the slim account bar sits above it all (see "How do I know which account I'm acting as?"). On a phone the two corners do different jobs. The **compass** is *where you can go*, the same on every page: Let's Explore, Get Local, Connect, Marketplace, the Trip planner, your Dashboard, and the Help Center (its big logo takes you home); not signed in, **Create account** and **Sign in** are at its top. The other corner (☰) is *what you can do here*: this part of the site's tools (tap a heading like Things to do to fold it open or shut), with **Dashboard** first so you can always get back. On pages with no tools of their own, like the FAQ, it isn't there. In your account, the layout switch (your full profile, or just your Pulse) sits in that corner too. Around the site the brown bar stays; in your account the page runs to the top with just those two corners. Notifications are in your Pulse (the bell there); an orange dot on the menu means something is waiting. To bring back the full logo or the row of icons, tap the small tab next to the compass or next to the menu. We remember your choice on this device.

### Do I need an account?
No, not to look around. You need one to save favorites, write journeys, comment, like, report something, or make suggestions under your name.

### How old do I have to be?
Traversence accounts are for adults: you must be 18 or older. You'll confirm it when you sign up. If your account was made before we asked, or you confirmed an earlier age, we'll ask once, the first time you comment or become a contributor.

### How do I choose where I'm looking?
Open the place picker at the top of the page (on the home page, **Change** under the search). You can:
- open the **Trip Planner** to plan a trip: where, when, how long and what you're after;
- pick a **region**;
- type a **destination**, or a **state, city or ZIP** anywhere in the U.S.;
- use **Auto-detect** to start near you;
- tap **Search everywhere** to have no place at all. In search, the **×** beside the place clears it the same way.

In **Destination** you can type a town, a town area, a local name, or a landmark like **Grand Canyon**, **Lyman Lake** or a museum. Towns come first. You can add the state after a comma ("St. Johns, AZ"); the list keeps up while you type it. Choosing a landmark takes you to the town area around it.

Signed in, you can make a place your default (**My default**), so you start there next time: whenever you're signed in and haven't chosen a place yet this visit, every page starts from it (shown as "your default"). A region you arrive through, or choosing everywhere, still wins for that visit.

### Does Auto-detect share where I am?
No. Auto-detect only picks a starting place for you on this visit. Your location is never shown to anyone.

## Exploring places

### Why does Let's Explore ask before showing my default place?
Your default place (the ZIP saved on your account) never picks the page by itself. Let's Explore offers it first: **Explore** the area it's in, **Choose another place**, or stay where you are. A place you choose yourself, or with Auto-detect, is followed straight away.

### How do I see more of a list?
Lists show 10 at a time; a list of cards side by side rounds that up to whole rows (four across shows 12), so a page never ends on a half row. **Show more** under a list adds the next ones; the bar under it has the page numbers, which ones you're seeing ("11–20 of 64"), and how many a page (10, 25, 50 or 100), which this browser remembers for that list. A list of 10 or fewer has neither. This works the same in the directory, Let's Explore, the Marketplace and Find groups.

### What's on the North America page?
The big picture: the story of the continent and its regions, plus the newest journeys and guides from everywhere. From there you can browse by **Regions**, **Experiences**, **Parks & Landmarks** or **Map**, or open the **Trip Planner**. **Map** shows what's around the place you've set, everything at first, then narrowed to what you'd do (Hike, Walk, Drive, Exhibits, On the water) or eat and stay; beside ours, the museums, galleries, gardens, parks, zoos, theatres and attractions OpenStreetMap knows there show as lighter pins (credited to OpenStreetMap), so the places people go are always on it; outside our areas it opens around you (or asks where first). **Parks & Landmarks** follows you outside our areas too: the landmarks near the place you've set, and the national parks nearest first, each with how far. It's where Let's Explore starts when you haven't chosen a place.

### How do I change the place I'm exploring?
Tap the pencil next to **Explore: <place>** at the top of Let's Explore or of any place page, and choose another place. On a place page, you go straight to the new place's page.

### Whose photos are at the top of a place?
Photos from journeys people have shared about that place, or about places inside it. A region shows photos from all its towns. Share a journey with photos and yours can appear there. Until there are any, a labelled stock photo may show instead (see [Why do some photos say Stock photo?](/faq.php#why-do-some-photos-say-stock-photo)).

### What are Experiences?
Things to do that are worth the trip: paddling a lake at sunrise, a rodeo weekend, a night under dark skies. You take part, it belongs to the place, and someone local can make it happen. Find them in **Let's Explore → Experiences**, in season first. Each one shows when to go, how long it takes, how hard it is, who can take you, and journeys from people who did it. An old link to traversence.com/experiences/ opens that list too.

### How do I post an experience?
Sign in and open **Dashboard → Your experiences**. Say what you do, where, what it's like and when to go, and add the local businesses that make it happen. You need to be 18 or older. Our editors review your first experiences before they show. If they don't approve one, you'll see their note and can edit it and send it again. Once two of yours have been approved, a well-described experience from you can go up at once.

### Where do the other experiences come from?
Some are posted by members. Some we suggest from what we already know: outdoor places and their activities, local guides, outfitters and museums, and journeys people shared. Our staff add others. Each one gets a credibility score from who posted it and how complete it is. The most credible go up at once and our editors check them afterwards. The rest wait for an editor.

### What's the difference between Experiences and National Parks?
National Parks are places. Experiences are what you do, in a park or anywhere else.

### What's on a place's own page?
Each place has its own guide: a **Regional guide** for a region, an **Area guide**, a **Community guide** for a group of towns, a **City guide** for a town and a **Neighborhood guide** for a local name. They all follow the same order: essentials, things to do, getting there, where to eat and stay, events, itineraries, what living there is like, and its local businesses. On a wide screen, at a glance, the places one step down (its towns, say) and landmarks & heritage run in a column beside the guide. A community of just one town (Farmington) opens that town's guide. A part with nothing in it yet is left out. In Let's Explore, **This place** in the ☰ menu opens it.

### How are places organized?
From large to small: North America, then regions, areas, communities (groups of towns), towns, and neighborhoods (the local names people use). Each has its own guide with its story, its journeys, and what's there.

### What do Visiting and Live here do on a guide?
They show the same guide in two orders. **Visiting** starts with the essentials and things to do, then what the place is like, getting there and where to eat. **Live here** starts with the essentials (who to call, road and weather alerts), local businesses and support. Your choice is remembered; coming from Get Local starts you on Live here.

### Where do a guide's weather and road conditions come from?
Weather and alerts come from the National Weather Service. Tap the weather by the guide's name to open the forecast (it starts folded): the next 24 hours as temperature, rain or wind, and the next seven days. It stays on the page; the weather service's own forecast is a link inside it. Arizona road work, lane closures, detours, incidents and alerts come from AZ 511 (its work zone feed uses the national WZDx format). Our server asks for the place's weather, not yours: your location is never sent. Weather updates every half hour, roads every ten minutes. Check AZ 511 before you drive.

### What is "Walking in the footsteps" in a guide?
The place's story, told where you can stand today: the peoples and families, trades, building materials, water and hard times that made it, each tied to a street, a building, a river crossing or a view you can visit. It's written from our heritage catalog: facts drawn from historic records (the Library of Congress's newspapers, photographs and maps, linked, never copied), the place's public records and our research, and only those backed by a historic record or two independent sources (several pages of one newspaper count as one). Archaeological, sacred and burial places follow the law of the place, at three levels. A place its managers invite visitors to (a state park's ruins tour, a museum, a town's public cemetery) is told like any other, with how to get there. One that public sources name but that isn't open to visitors has its story told, never where it is or how to reach it, with a reminder that it may be visited only with the authorization of the nation or the land's managers. One no public source names isn't named at all; a guide may say only that the area holds places sacred to a nation and that they need its permission. On a tribal nation's land nothing is told until the nation says. Where a record's source asks to be credited (the Library of Congress), it's named under the chapter, and a fact we may only use to confirm something, never to tell it, isn't shown. Every guide shows when it was first published and when it was last checked. We check each guide every month, and sooner when something changes: a place it names closes, someone suggests a change, new historic records turn up, the season turns, or many more people than usual start reading it.

### Where do the events under "Coming up" come from?
From each event's own announcement. We read the town's or county's own events and calendar pages first and, for what they don't list, look on the web, keeping only events with a date ahead and a page that announces them. Each shows with its dates, time, where it is (linked when it's a place listed with us), its price when the page gives one, and a link to that page: check it before you go, since plans change. An event shows in the guide's Events section (a community's guide includes its towns') and on its venue's page under **Coming up here**, until the day after it ends. A ceremony or feast at a sacred place is listed only when the nation or parish announces it as open to the public, never with directions, and anything whose name may mark a protected place is read by a person first. We're switching this on place by place, so some guides don't show events yet. Something wrong or missing? Tap **Suggest a change** on the guide.

### What does a highlighted name in a guide do?
Point at it (tap it on a phone) and a small card shows what it is, where it is, its phone number if it has one, and **See more**, which opens its page. Every business, landmark and outdoor place a guide names is highlighted this way, and so are the names in journeys, place stories, experiences and how-tos, so you can go deeper without losing your place. You can also search for any of them by name: a landmark, park or museum a guide names is in search as soon as the guide has it.

### Why are names in my journey highlighted?
When you publish a journey (or an experience), we look for the places and businesses it names that are on Traversence, link them, and your piece shows under **Featured in** on their pages. If you've turned on AI Assist & Learning and have a paid plan (your own, or one on a business you own), Claude reads your words to find the names; if not, we only match names from our own directory, and nothing you wrote leaves Traversence. Right after you publish, and any time on your journey's page or its editor, you'll see what it's tagged with: tap × on any that aren't right and it comes off at once. Only you see that list. A name we don't have yet isn't added on its own: a person checks it first.

### What is "Featured in" on a listing or a landmark?
The published guides, journeys, place stories, experiences and how-tos that name it, so you can read what's said about it there. Group posts never show here: they're for the group's members. Nobody pays to be named.

### What do I get when I follow a business or a place?
Besides its own posts: when a guide, journey or story names a business you follow, or a place you follow gets a new guide or story, it shows in your feeds and your Pulse. A member's journey that names a business reaches that business's followers only if they also follow the person who wrote it or the place it's about.

### Who hears about it when I @mention someone?
The person you mention, once, in their Pulse, if they're in the group you posted in (so they can read it). Nobody else is told, and your post doesn't show anywhere outside the group.

### What's in a guide's Essentials?
Weather alerts and road conditions, who to call, and the nearest places for what you might need, three of each, nearest first. Everyone sees emergency and urgent care, pharmacies, police, fire and ambulance, fuel and charging, groceries, banks and ATMs, and visitor information and libraries. **Visiting** adds car trouble, getting around, pet emergencies, and laundry, mail and shipping. **Live here** adds the town hall and government offices, utilities and trash, schools and childcare, doctors, dentists and counseling, home repairs, and community help. **See more** opens the full list in Get Local. For an emergency, call 911.

### How do you choose the businesses a guide shows?
By what the section is about, how close they are, the kind of place (a quick bite or a sit-down meal), the season (winter leads with snow sports, summer with the lakes) and the weather right now: when rain, a storm warning, a heat wave, a deep cold or a strong wind keeps people in, museums, galleries and other indoor places come first, with a **Right now** note saying why; on a fair day the outdoors leads. Places our writers name come first. A paid plan never moves a business up. We only show public listings that are open, one per business: a business marked closed drops out of every guide.

### Where do I find a place's guide?
On the place's own page (open the place from search or Regions), and in **Let's Explore → Stories & guides**, which lists the guides for the place you're searching and the towns in it. A place without one says so; guides are added as they're written and checked.

### Who writes the guides?
Traversence compiles them. Our crawler first gathers what's public about the place, free: its Wikipedia article (read for facts, never copied), its facts on Wikidata, its town or county's own website, the places near it on the National Register of Historic Places, the landmarks and outdoor places we hold nearby, and the Library of Congress's records of it, including pages of historic newspapers (Chronicling America) that name it. A new guide waits up to two days for these, so its history is drawn from them first. Claude, the AI we use, then researches the place in depth, told what those records already hold so it searches only for what they don't (its peoples and founding, the families and events that shaped it, its faith, food and crafts, the festivals of its year and what it's like to be there) and keeps that research with the place. It writes the guide from, first, what people on the ground know (our team's notes from being there, and the journeys and experiences people have published about the place, a member's only if they've turned on AI & Learning), then that history, then what visitors search for there (counts only, never who). It searches the web only for what the crawler didn't find, and names only places it can back up. What the crawler gathers is kept with the place, so it's never fetched twice, and its facts (when the town was founded, its population) show under At a glance. Each guide gets a confidence score, from how sure Claude is, what it was written from, its sources and how many of its names we have. When the score is high enough, the guide goes live by itself, marked "Compiled by Traversence from public sources and local knowledge" with the date, and our team corrects or takes it down whenever something's off. Every month each live guide is checked again, the ones people have been reading first: whether what the crawler knows about the place changed, whether a date in it has passed, whether a place it names has closed, and whether its research is a year old. When something has, the parts Claude wrote are written again and scored the same way; anything our team wrote is flagged for them instead. If all that's wrong is a date that has passed, a business that has closed or a link that no longer works, just those sentences are corrected (by the least costly AI, checked before it's kept) rather than rewriting the guide. See something wrong? Tap **Suggest a change** on the guide.

### What are suggested journeys on a guide?
Routes through the guide's own places, under **Suggested journeys**, labelled "Suggested by Traversence". The guide decides what kinds there are: whatever runs most strongly through it (the outdoors, history and heritage, food, the arts or shopping) becomes a journey, and a guide with enough of everything also gets "A day in" the place. Each stop is one the guide recommends, in an order that makes sense to travel, and timed: breakfast in the morning, lunch at midday, a museum while it's open, a trail or viewpoint in daylight. Between stops you see how long it takes to drive or walk and how far; "about" means it's our estimate. Tap **See the stops** for the list, the arrow beside each for directions, or the route icon to send the whole journey to Google Maps or Apple Maps (see below). They're timed for a Saturday, so check hours on other days. They're built by rules from what we know, with no AI, and rebuilt every week or when the guide changes, so a place that closes drops out. A business is in a journey because it fits the route, never because it pays. Tap **Make it my trip** to copy it into a trip of your own and change it (see "How do I save places to a trip?").

### How do I save places to a trip?
On a place, a business or an outdoor place, tap ⋯ then **Add to a trip**, and pick one of your trips or **New trip** (named for the place, like "Trip to Show Low"). On **Get Local** it's in each card's ⋯. On a ready-made journey, **Make it my trip** copies its stops into a trip of your own. It says "Added to Trip to Show Low" with **Open it** and **Undo**. Your trips are under **Your trips** in your account's menu (and beside the Trip Planner on Let's Explore): take a stop out (with Undo), rename a trip, send it to Google Maps or Apple Maps, or delete it (it's kept 30 days, so you can put it back). Each trip has its own page (see "What's on a trip's page?"); sharing a trip and publishing it as a guide come after.

### What's on a trip's page?
Open a trip from **Your trips** (or **Open it** after adding a place). Pick the day it **starts**, how many **days**, and when **each day starts**. Stops are listed by day, in order: drag one to move it (or use ↑ ↓, and the day beside it, on a phone), and choose how long you'll **stay** at each. Every stop gets a time, worked out from the drive before it; with a date, each is checked against its hours that day ("Closed when you'd arrive: Opens 11 AM", with **Move to day 2**), and parks and outdoor places against daylight. Times are in the time at the trip's first stop, always labelled (MST, PDT…); a stop in another time zone shows its own. The map numbers the stops, one colour a day, and draws the chosen day's route in lime; tap **Day 1**, **Day 2**… over the map, or one of a day's stops, to see another. The miles and times between stops are straight-line estimates, so they say "about"; driving routes on our own map are coming. **Send to Maps** sends the whole trip to Google Maps or Apple Maps for turn-by-turn directions, and **Print** prints the list.

### Can I start a trip from the Trip Planner?
Yes. On Let's Explore's **Trip Planner**, pick where, when and how long, then **Save as a trip**: it becomes a trip with those days, and its page offers the ready-made journeys for that place as a start (**Use these stops**). Searching for ideas works as before, and **Add to a trip** is on a place's **Peek** in the results and on the map's card.

### Who can see my trips?
Only you, unless you share it. A trip is private: nobody sees who added a place to a trip. On a trip's page, **Share** makes a link: anyone with it can view the trip (its days, times and map), not change it, and it shows nothing about you. **Turn off** stops the link at once; sharing again makes a new link. Stops that can't be shown publicly (a place whose location is kept private, one marked closed) are left out of a shared trip. You can also publish a trip as a Personal Guide (see below), only when you choose. Deleting your account deletes your trips.

### What's a Personal Guide?
A trip you publish for everyone: on a trip's page, **Publish as a guide**, give it a title and a few sentences about it, and **Publish**. It's credited to you, like a journey, and it changes as your trip does. You need to be a contributor first (**Your journeys → Become a contributor**: 18 or older, with a public profile). Your words get the same automated check as a journey's: if something is held, it says why and you can change it. Readers can like it, comment and report it, and our team can hide it, telling you why. **Withdraw** takes it down; **Publish again** puts it back. Places whose location is kept private never show in it. If you delete your account, your guides are taken down and their words removed.

### Can a business see who added it to a trip?
No. A business sees only how many times it was added to a trip ("Added to trips" in its insights, with its calls and directions), never who. A place, a landmark or an outdoor place shows it on its page, beside its views ("Added to 12 trips this month"), never who. Nobody on our team is counted.

### Why can't I add some places to a trip?
**Add to a trip** is greyed, with the reason, when a place can't go in one: a place whose location is kept private (a shelter: call them instead), a place we tell about but don't put on the map, a business closed for good, or one with only a mailbox address. A stop that becomes one of these later stays in your trip, greyed with the reason, and is left out when you send the trip to Maps.

### Can I make a trip without an account?
Yes: as a guest, one trip is kept on this device (in this browser only). Make an account to keep it and see it anywhere: when you sign in, the trip on this device moves into your account. Private browsing may not keep it.

### How do I send a whole journey to Google Maps or Apple Maps?
Tap the route icon on a suggested journey, or **Send to Maps** under "Make a day of it" on a place's page. Pick **Google Maps** or **Apple Maps** and where to start: **My location** or the first stop. Your choices are kept on this device for next time. Every stop goes, in order. One link holds ten points, so a longer route is split into legs ("Leg 1 of 3"): open them in order, each starting where the last one ended. Nothing is left off. Several stops in Apple Maps need iOS 18.4 or macOS 15.4 or later. It's a plain link: we send nothing to Google or Apple, and it costs nothing. A trip you've saved goes the same way, from **Your trips**.

### Why do more names in a guide link now, even in places you don't cover?
When a guide names a beach, park, museum or historic place we don't have yet, we look for it in Wikidata, the free public database behind Wikipedia, near the guide's place. When it's there, it gets its own page with us (what it is, its story from Wikipedia, a free photo when we find one, and the guides and journeys that name it), and the name in the guide links to it. Journeys can stop there too. We also look on our own: around each of our pilot towns (once a year), across each pilot state bit by bit (each month), and for any name people search for that we don't have yet ("Four Corners Monument", "Laguna Art Museum"), anywhere in the US. Museums, monuments, historic sites, harbors and parks with a Wikipedia article or on the National Register of Historic Places get a page with us and can be found by name, usually within minutes of the first search. Where a place is (its town and state) comes from the same source. So does what it is: each one is filed under one of our categories (Museums, Parks, Churches…) by what its source says it is, never guessed from its name, so tapping its kind shows more like it and a search like "museums near me" finds it. Until a category fits, it shows the source's own word for it ("Historic district"). Only the words of a search are looked up, never who searched, and never a search with a number or an email address in it. Businesses aren't added this way: they go through the same checks as every listing. A place that may be sacred, a burial site or otherwise protected is never named or located.

### Can I get directions to a place a guide names?
Yes. Tap or hover a highlighted name: its card shows what it is, a map pin to see it on our map, and an arrow for directions in your own maps app (Google Maps). **See more** opens its page.

### Where do the travel times come from?
From OpenStreetMap's roads, through a free routing service (openrouteservice), asked once for the places in a town and then kept with us, so the next journey, trip or card reads our own record instead of asking again. They're renewed only when a place moves, someone tells us a route is wrong, or the time is two years old. When we don't have one yet, we estimate it from the straight-line distance and say "about".

### Is everything named in a guide real?
Every business, place, landmark and road a guide names that we have links to its page with us. When a guide names something we don't have yet, it shows as plain text while we look for it: first on the map (OpenStreetMap) and its own website, then by one web search for its own website, and only if that finds nothing, by Claude on official sources. What we find gets a confidence score: when it's high and the place has an address, it joins the listings our crawler finds and goes through the same checks (a real place, a way to reach it, a category that fits, and not listed already). Once it passes, it's published and the name links, usually within the quarter hour; anything the checks can't settle, our team looks at. A shelter or safe house is never looked up outside: a person links it. A place that shares its phone or website with a listing under another name (a county office's line) is always checked by a person. A guide never names a place that may be sacred, burial or protected, or a business that's closed: it waits as a draft until those are taken out.


### How do I ask you to take something down?
Tap **Ask us to take this down** at the foot of any section of a guide, on a landmark's page, or under a topic in AI Assist. You don't need an account. Tell us why: if it shows the way to a sacred, burial or archaeological place, if you speak for a tribal nation and it shouldn't be told, if it shows someone's private information, or if it puts someone or something at risk, it comes down the moment you send it. Anything else (it copies your work, it's wrong) comes down within 24 hours while a person looks, sooner if they finish first. In the "Walking in the footsteps" chapter you can name the one fact you mean, and the chapter stays out until it's written again without it. Leave your email and we'll tell you what we decided: it stays down, or it's back because we found nothing wrong or fixed it. What you tell us is used only to decide and is never shown. Each quarter our team also reads a random sample of published guides against the same standard. For a member's post, journey or comment, use its **Report** button instead.

### Why does a landmark's page have no map or directions?
Some places matter to the people whose history they hold: a name that speaks of the sacred, of burials or of archaeology. When a place like that is publicly known (it has its own Wikipedia article, or is open to visitors) and isn't on a tribal nation's land, we tell its story, never the way there: its page has its name, what it is and its story, with no pin, map, directions, distance or nearest town, and it isn't on any map, nearby list or search pin. If it's open to visitors, its managing authority (a park, a museum, a nation) is the place to ask. It shows fully only when a person on our team adds the managing authority's own page saying it welcomes visitors. A place on a nation's land, or one no public source names, isn't told at all until the nation says. See something that shouldn't be shown? Tap **Ask us to take this down** on its page.

### I searched for a town with no guide. What does "Build the guide" do?
When you search somewhere we don't have a guide for yet (Dana Point, California, say), search offers to build one, under the results. Tap **Build the guide** and a page shows each step as it happens: we find the places there (places to eat and stay from OpenStreetMap, outdoor places and landmarks), build suggested journeys from them, and Claude, the AI we use, writes a first look from public sources. It takes about two minutes, and you can leave the page: it carries on, and the guide is there when you come back. If a step fails (a service we use is busy), it's tried again by itself after half an hour, two hours and eight hours; if it still can't be built, our team is told and looks at it. Want to hear when it's ready? Tap **Notify me when it's ready**: signed in, you get a notice in your Pulse and an email; signed out, type your email and we write once (we don't keep the address after). If the page is still open in another tab, your browser can tell you too. The first look is marked **First look** while we keep researching; within a day or two the full guide follows, with the place's history from the Library of Congress, old newspapers and deeper research, and the label comes off once it passes the same checks as every guide. Everything gathered is kept, so the next person to search that place gets it straight away. Every place a guide names is checked against our sources, and a place that may be sacred or protected is never named. Building the guide also starts our crawler on the town: it uses OpenStreetMap as its lead for everything it has there (places to eat and stay, groceries and gas, things to do, shops, services like banks, salons and auto repair, public services like the library and town hall, places of worship, campgrounds and health care), checks each one on its own website, and a place confirmed by both becomes a listing (the rest wait for our team to check), usually within an hour or two, so the town's search results, guide and journeys fill in with real listings. Its trailheads, parks and viewpoints are looked up on Wikidata by name, and the ones with a Wikipedia article or on the National Register get a page of their own with us. Writing costs us money, so we write a set number of new guides a day; when that's used, a place waits until the next day (the most-asked first), and you still see its places and journeys straight away. Finding places takes under a minute or so; when OpenStreetMap is slow, the step carries on with what it has and the rest is filled in later, so it never sits on Finding places. **Email me when it's ready** answers at once, even while a step is running.


### Why does a guide I asked for say new guides are paused?
Now and then we pause building new guides (to check how our tools gather information, for example). While they're paused, search still shows everything we already list for the place, and asking for a guide still counts: we keep your ask, and once guides are back on they're built in order, the most asked-for places first. Tap **Notify me when it's ready** to hear when yours is up (in your Pulse and by email when you're signed in, or once to the email you type), or tap **See what we list near** the place to look around now. Questions? Write to us at support@traversence.com.
### Why isn't there a guide for my state yet?
We open one state at a time. Before a state opens, its laws on archaeological sites, burials and privacy are written up and reviewed with an attorney, so we know what may be told there and what must be held back. Until then its guides don't go live and we don't gather its history. Arizona and New Mexico are open; others are coming.

### I searched for a local name (like Wide Ruins) and landed on a town. Why?
Many small communities are known by local names that aren't on maps as towns. We file each local name under the town area it belongs to, so searching it shows that town area's places. **Get Local**'s Map shows them on the map.

### On a place page, what happens when I tap the kind, the agency or an activity?
It searches for more like it around there. Tapping **Campground** finds other campgrounds nearby. Tapping the managing agency finds other places it runs nearby, and tapping **Fishing** finds more places to fish. The search shows listings, outdoor places, stories and groups together.

### Can I link a lake, campground or trail?
Yes. Use the link icon in the corner of its page. It's saved with your other linked places.

### Can I link a journey?
Yes, once it's published. Use the link icon in the corner of its page, or on its card. It's saved with your other linked places. A draft, or a journey hidden after reports, can't be linked.

### What does clicking a town name do?
It opens the directory for that town, so you can switch between the list and the map.

### When I choose a town, why can I set how far to look, and what is its "Town area"?
Choosing a town (like Show Low, AZ) centres the search on the town itself, within 10 miles to start; **Within** lets you look closer or farther. Many of our towns also have a **Town area**, the town with the towns around it (The Show Low District, for one): choose that when you want the whole area, with no distance.

### How do tribal lands appear?
Tribal nations are sovereign nations, and we present them that way. We show public places and public events only. We never point to sacred or restricted sites.

## The directory (Get Local)

### Can I contact a business that isn't on Traversence yet?
Yes. **Call**, **Email**, **Website** and **Directions** open on your own device (we count the tap, never who). Or use **Leave a message** on its page: if the listing gives an email, we send your message there as a visitor's request (your email and phone are never shown) and bring any reply back to you; if not, we keep it on the listing and deliver it only if the business claims it. Messages never claimed are deleted after 90 days, and we tell you.

### What do I need to leave a message?
An account, or an email we confirm by a link first, or a phone number you let the business have once it claims the listing. Without one the message can't be left. Guests get a reply code: keep it, it's the only way back to your message; **Check a reply** on the listing's page shows where it stands.

### How do I get back to where I was?
Pages you reach from **Let's Explore** (a region, a place, a listing, a guide, a journey) show **‹ Back to …** at the top: in your account bar when you're signed in, otherwise in a slim line atop the page. **▾** beside it shows the whole route you took, and opening a page already on it takes you back up. A listing, place or guide opened some other way (from Pulse, the admin, or a link elsewhere on the site) shows **‹ Back** to the page you came from (2026-11-20).

### Can I take a message back?
Yes. **Withdraw** it any time before it's delivered, and it's deleted along with your email.

### Why did my business get a "visitors have left you messages" email?
Visitors left messages for a listing we show from public information. The first request comes at once; after that, one notice when 5 are waiting, or after 10 days. Every email has **Stop requests to this address** and **Report this as spam**. Claim your listing (free) to read and answer them all, with their history.


### What is "Help us find this business"?
Some businesses we know only by their name and their town: we don't yet have a phone number or an address we can check. Instead of listing them half-finished, we ask. They show on their town's page and in search under **Help us find these businesses**, never as listings (no map pin, no Call or Directions). If you know one, tap **Know its phone or address?** and tell us what you know and how you know it (you shop there, its sign, its Facebook page). Our team checks it, and once we have a phone or a street address, it becomes a listing. If it's your business, tap **Is it yours? Claim it**. We never show a person's name this way, or the name of a protected place, and a request nobody answers in six months is set aside (an answer brings it back).
### What is a "Mail & package drop-off"?
A place to drop off mail or a package without going inside: a USPS blue collection box, a UPS or FedEx drop box, a package locker. We list them as their own kind, **Mail & package drop-off**, with a pin on the map, so you can find the nearest one: search "drop box near" a town, or open the map. Search also shows the boxes OpenStreetMap knows about, credited to OpenStreetMap. They aren't businesses, so they stay out of the directory's business lists and aren't counted in a town's or an area's number of places (on its page and in the place finder), there's nothing to call and nothing to claim; check the box itself for its pickup times. Something wrong, or a box gone? Tap **Suggest an edit** on it.

### Why do some kinds of business have different names than I expected?
We show each kind of business by the name people use, so you'll see "Doctors" rather than "Physicians & Surgeons" and "Self
Storage" rather than "Storage-Household & Commercial". Where a few of the trade headings mean the same thing, they show as one,
and choosing it shows every business in all of them. Searching by either name works.

### How do I find a business in Get Local?
Get Local opens with one bar, as the home page has: **what you're looking for** (a business's name, the kind of business, or what you need: "pizza", "oil change", "Head Start"), **where** (a town or ZIP, or tap the target for where you are), and the search button. As you type, it offers what the words could mean, the kind of business first ("Barber shops · near St. Johns", or "· near me"), then the businesses themselves; the list also follows what you type once you pause. Under the bar, one line says what you're seeing and lets you change it: "**24 businesses** within **any distance** of **St. Johns**". Tap **businesses** to pick a kind, **any distance** to choose how far (2, 5, 10, 25 or 50 miles, or any), or the place for the towns in these results or somewhere else; a kind or words you searched show there with × to clear them. On the right of that line: **Filters** (Open now, Has a phone, Has a website, ★ Favorites; one that's on shows in the line with × to turn it off), **Arrange** (see "How do I arrange Get Local's results?") and **Sort**. Each business is numbered, so you can match it to its pin on the map.

### How do I search the directory?
Use the search bar at the top of any page. The panel that opens has:
- **Location** at the top: the place you're searching. Tap ✎ to change it.
- **Search Directory**: search **All**, or just **Discovery**, the **Directory**, **Community** or the **Marketplace**.
- **Distance**: slide from **My place only** to **∞** (no limit), or pick a distance like 25 miles. It starts at **10 mi** around your place and goes back to 10 mi whenever you change your place, so search doesn't look outside your place unless you widen it. Your place stays as you set it: searching, tapping a town in search, or opening another area's page never changes it. That page shows its area, and your place changes only when you choose a new one (or tap **Make it my place**). A link to an area sets your place only when you haven't chosen one yet. With no place chosen there's nothing to measure from, so the slider rests at ∞ until you choose one.
- **Search Results** on the left. Each one says which part of the site it's in: **Directory**, **Discovery** or **Community**, and the tabs over them use the same names ("Discovery 1", "Directory 4"). The tabs count only what's listed under them, so they add up to the Results count; the categories your words might mean are in Suggestions.
- **Suggestions** under them, one per line: the categories that match a word you typed ("All Churches in Saint Johns"), each word on its own when nothing has them all, and other searches for the place. When your words describe a situation, its answer already says what kind of place helps, so no category lines show.
- **Quick Search** on the right: your own quick searches and one-tap searches for the place, each with how many it finds where you are (**Open now** has no number: it changes by the minute; nor does one that finds nothing there), and the filters (**Near me**, **Open now**, **Has a phone**, **Has a website**, **Favorites**). **Recent Searches** are under them.
- **Show in directory list** opens your results as a list.

### How does search read what I type?
As you type, it guesses where you're headed: "plum" in Show Low offers **Plumbers** there first, "coffee near" offers **Coffee near me** and coffee near your place, "place to take my k" offers places that suit kids, and the first letters of a town ("pho") offer the town. Some other things it does:
- **The full name of a place wins.** "Four Corners Monument", "Laguna Art Museum" or "Fool Hollow Lake" finds that place, not the town that shares part of its name.
- **A misspelled town is offered, never changed.** "flagstaf" asks "Did you mean flagstaff?", and tapping it searches Flagstaff, AZ.
- **"Near me" stays inside your Distance.** For everyday errands (coffee, gas, urgent care), ours are shown within the Distance you set, and OpenStreetMap fills in where we don't list enough yet. Places to go, like trails and parks, still reach 25 miles.

### Can I search for sports, like football or basketball?
Yes. **Sports** finds athletic fields, stadiums, gyms, golf courses, public pools, rodeos and sports clubs nearby. **Football**, **basketball**, **baseball**, **soccer** and other sports also find high schools, where most local games are played, and ball fields find parks. Try **gym**, **swimming**, **tennis**, **hunting** or **fishing** too. If a place you play or watch is missing, tap **Tell us what's missing**.

### Can I find natural places like badlands, arches and canyons?
Yes. Search for words like **badlands**, **arch**, **crater**, **hot springs**, **waterfall** or **canyon**, alone or with a place ("crater near Flagstaff"). Each landmark has its own page with a photo where there's a freely licensed one, a short description from Wikipedia, directions, and comments. Names and locations come from USGS Geographic Names. Some landmarks, like monuments and viewpoints, aren't in that data, and our team adds them by hand. If you know one that's missing, use **Tell us** on any page.

### Why don't some landmarks on tribal land show?
Tribal nations are sovereign nations, and each decides what visitors are welcome to see on its land. On a nation's land we show only its public places, the ones the nation itself lists for visitors (like Four Corners Monument), and public businesses. Other places there aren't shown. That goes for the open map data we show (OpenStreetMap) too: on a nation's land, only its businesses show, and a trailhead, viewpoint or reserve there waits for the nation. We never show the location of sacred or restricted sites, and anything a nation asks us to close is closed at once. A guide may name a place the public already knows (a public source publishes it) but that isn't open to visit, and tell its story, but never how to get there, and it says the place may be visited only with the authorization of the nation or the land's managers. A place a nation has closed, or that we hold, is never named.

### Where do I find parks and landmarks?
In **Let's Explore**, open **Parks & Landmarks**. National parks, monuments and historic sites come first. Under them are natural landmarks like arches, buttes, canyons and falls: the ones near the place you're exploring, or, with no place chosen, the best-known ones with photos. They show 24 at a time; **Show more landmarks** under them adds the next 24, and goes away when there are no more. Tap one for its page. The parks shown are the ones near the place you're exploring (within 150 miles of its towns, nearest first, with the miles; one closer than half a mile says "under ½ mi", and one we know only by its town or ZIP shows no distance rather than a wrong one), never the whole country by name. Each park's card shows a photo of that park: its own photos first, else the Wikimedia Commons photo of the landmark of the same name (credited on the card), else a Commons photo found for the park itself (taken there and named for it, credited), else a stock photo of the kind of place. Each photo's credit is written on the photo itself: who took it and its licence ("© Ann Lee · CC BY-SA 4.0"), or **Stock photo** for a representative stock photo of the kind of place, with an **i** (point at it, or press and hold, for the full credit). The same stock photo never shows twice in one list, and a memorial or monument never gets one: it shows its own photo, a Commons photo, or the pattern. Only when there's none of those does it show our map pattern. A public place, like a state park or a memorial, shows the agency that runs it ("Run by Arizona State Parks & Trails") rather than "Unclaimed". Data from the National Park Service itself is coming.

### How do I switch between my accounts or roles?
If you help run a business, or you're on the Traversence team, you can act as yourself or in that role. Tap **your photo** at the left of the account bar, the slim bar at the very top of every page. The list shows, in order: your **Traversence** staff account if you have one, **you** (your Personal Account), then **your listings** just under you, each one tap to switch to it and open its Overview, where all its tools are. With more than five, **See all** opens every one in a pop-up with a search box. **All your accounts** opens a page with all of them, a brand's locations grouped together. In the bar itself, the account's name opens its dashboard, and ✎ beside it edits it: your profile when you're yourself, the listing's page when you're acting as a business. It works the same on a phone and a computer.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do my staff login and my own account fit together?
They're separate: the staff login (a staff ID like TRV-0001, your work email and a password) opens the admin side; your own account is for everything else. An owner links them for you from the admin (People → Staff logins → Link a member account), and we email your own account to confirm: sign in as it, open the link and tap **Link them**. You can unlink from your own account (Dashboard → Privacy → Staff login) at any time. After you sign in with the staff login, the account menu switches to your own account and back without signing in again, for up to 12 hours after you last used the admin side in that browser; after that, the switch back asks you to sign in with the staff login again. While you're on the admin side, the menu's **Your listings** shows your own account's listings: tapping one switches to your own account and opens it. Signing in with your own account never opens the admin side.

### I'm on the Traversence team and can't get into my work email. How do I sign in?
Sign in at the staff sign-in (https://traversence.com/admin/login.php) with your staff ID and password as usual. On the code step, choose **Can't get the email?** and give your name and a phone number or another email where we can reach you. It goes to the owners as a case in Pulse; nothing changes on your login until one of them has contacted you to check it's you. Then they can change your work email, reset your work mailbox's password, or make a new setup link and hand it to you in person or by phone (never only by email, since that's the mailbox you can't reach). Every step is kept in your staff log, and your old work email is told when it's changed. Forgot your password as well? Ask an owner directly, or write to support@traversence.com. Once you're back in, add an authenticator app (People → Staff logins → Authenticator app, https://traversence.com/admin/staff.php#my-app): the admin side reminds you once until you add it or choose **Not now**, and its code signs you in even when email is slow or out of reach.

### I'm on the Traversence team. Where do I see what our outside services cost, and their limits?
**System Configuration → Modules (Service register)** has a row for every outside service we call (Claude, Google Places, Tavily, Amazon SES, OpenStreetMap, the free registries…): which workflows use it, where its key lives (named, never shown), its caps and paces (the one setting the site reads, kept with who changed it and when, its history and a Reset), this month's use and cost by our count, and its status with the reason (active, paced, held, off, or out of budget). A service with an open issue shows it on its card: what came back, how many times and since when, from where, what it means and what to do, with the button that does it: **Check and start again** when it tripped, else **Check it now** (a service that asked us to slow down just waits its turn), and **Its case in Pulse** (2026-11-19; since 2026-11-20 the issue is the alarm itself, one thread, so there's no separate "Its alarm on the board"). There's no Mark resolved: an issue is resolved when its check passes, the result of it being fixed. When it's the service's own server failing, there's nothing to change on our side: the check asks once, and if it answers, what waited carries on. **Costs** puts this month's spend together, by service and by workflow, against last month's revenue and the community budget, with the AI cap's share per workflow. Pulse opens one case per service at 50%, 75% and 95% of a cap, with the step that fixes it as a button (raise the cap, top up, pause the work), and one case for tasks that **return empty** (they had work and got answers, but nothing came back). Each workflow (W0 to W14) is a gauge card on **System Configuration → Workflows**, opening its own page with its last run, output and cost, and every setting it has (its tasks' schedule and limits, the services each may use, its budgets, gates, score weights, retry ladder and the steps that need a person); each save is a version with **Go back to it**. **Check before switching on**, now in the System Control Board's Start-up stage, lists what must be green before automation runs again, line by line like the pre-flight: each line a ✓ or ✗ that opens to its answer and its fix, with **Run the check** (on the server, one line at a time, so you can leave the page), **Show only what's red**, **Show the slow ones** and the run log with each run's report. It's read each time the page opens and at each Start, and the same lines are a group of the pre-flight, so the board, Start and the pre-flight always agree. A line that holds everything keeps the master switch off; any other red line holds only its own part (a hub's licence sources, for instance, hold only W14, the workflow that reads them). Watching Railway's deploys and the sync from the site is built but switched off until its keys are added; its panel, with its switch, is on the **System Control Board** (Watching Railway and the sync). **Start the system** (System Configuration → Automation, and the Workflow board) does the whole switch-on in one press, on the server, so the page can close: it checks the keys, switches on watching, runs the check and fixes what it safely can, sets the crawler's schedule on Railway, turns the master switch on, then starts W1 to W12 one at a time and then the new tasks, each only after the one before has run cleanly (2 good runs or 6 hours by default, no new service issue, nothing out of budget). A missing key or a red line holds only what it's for, and becomes a step in Pulse's **Starting the system** case; fix it and the start carries on by itself. **Pause everything** turns the master off and stops Railway's schedule in one press. The Start card shows its steps as a queue, like the pre-flight: one line per step with a ✓ when done, a spinner while it runs, ! when held and a dash while it waits its turn, with the counts on top; while it runs, the card follows it without a reload. It stands on its own: each step opens to what holds it and its fix right there (a button where the site can do it, the exact page where only you can) and **Check again**, which looks again at once and carries on if it's clear; each workflow and task opens to **Started**, **Running** (its good runs so far), **Complete** (when it passed its gate) and **Active status** (on or paused now, and its last run). The **run log** has one line per Start (how it ended, when it started and ended, who pressed it, its counts), and each opens to its report: every step with where it ended up, then every event of that Start in order. The pre-flight is a separate, deeper check, linked from the card; Start doesn't wait on it. Start first confirms that every workflow and task is operational, within minutes, the way the pre-flight tests them (its services, keys, scheduled jobs, gate, and a dry run where there is one), shown on the card as **Confirm each workflow and task is operational**; one that isn't is held with its answer and fix, and the rest go on. Then the master switch comes on and the confirmed ones switch on in order straight away, each new task once its workflow is on, so nothing that others rely on is left off for hours. Each one is then watched by itself (two good runs or six hours by default, in **Thresholds & gates**). A workflow stops at its gate only for a real hold on a service it uses (a block, a refused key, a used-up quota, or every server down with nothing else to answer), not for one server timing out while others answer. A stopped step carries its fixes on the card itself: **Try it again**, **Leave it paused, carry on**, and for a service issue its one button: **Check and start again** when it tripped, else **Check issue now** (a passing check closes it; **It's fixed: try it again** only where nothing can check it); once the issue no longer holds the service, **Check again** carries on by itself. Pulse gets a notice, never the only way to fix it. A fix is always done on the card itself: a hub's licence sources are listed under W14, each with **Read its terms** (the source's own terms, opened beside the page) and **Terms read: allowed**; any other page a fix needs opens in a drawer over the card, never by leaving it. Every press on the card brings you back to the card, with its answer at the top of it. A workflow someone paused after its turn shows as **Paused** with **Switch it on**. A line held by a service's problem says on the card what happened and what to do: when it's the service's own server failing, nothing (the site asks again by itself, and **Check issue now** tries at once); when it refused us or our key, what a person does, then its button once it's sorted: a passing check is what closes it, and it closes everywhere at once (the card, the board, Pulse and the logbook), saying how (2026-11-19). A stopped step also says **What to do**: the one button that fits, and why. While it's confirming or switching things on, a Start reads **Starting**; once everything confirmed is on it reads **Operational**, with how many are on, still watched, held or stopped. Each Start in the run log has **Download report** (a text file) and **Open** (the same report as a page you can read, save or share, on a phone too). A workflow held only by its own gate, such as W14 waiting for a hub's licence terms to be read, shows amber (it waits) and doesn't count against launch. Before it, **System Configuration → Pre-flight** tests every service, setting, workflow and task; **Run all** runs on the server, so it carries on if you leave the page, and the page shows how far it has got when you come back (a run the server cut off waits for you: the page asks whether to continue it from where it stopped or cancel it, and Pulse has both as buttons). Every run, finished or not, is one line in the run log with its status, its start and end, who started it, and its report as far as it got.

At its top, **Costs this month** (2026-11-20) opens to the whole month's spend: against last month's revenue and the community budget, by service, by workflow, the AI cap's shares and a hub's opening budget. It was its own page; https://traversence.com/admin/config/costs opens it here.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What are Switches and test accounts?
**System Configuration → Switches** turns a new feature on or off with no upload: first for staff and test accounts, then a share of members, then everyone, with a kill switch that turns it off for everyone at once. **System Configuration → Test accounts** is where the Founder makes the accounts our automatic checks sign in as (a member, a contributor, a business team on a hidden test listing, and staff logins at each level). Test accounts are left out of counts, likes, emails, people search and billing, the site sends them nothing, and they can only pay in sandbox mode.

### Do you try changes before they reach the site?
Yes. Changes are being set up to go first to a private copy of the whole site, where we try them with every kind of account before they reach you. The copy never sends anyone email, never takes a real payment, isn't shown in search engines and needs a password to open. When we refresh it from the live site, names, email addresses, phone numbers, street addresses and private messages are replaced with made-up test values first, so your details never go there. Running each new release through it first is coming.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What is staging?
**System Configuration → Staging** is the copy of the site at staging.trvgo.us. It's the live site in safe mode: it acts like live, and nothing done there reaches the real world until it's published. The Founder sets it up once (**Set up staging**, which also gives the test accounts new sign-ins, shown once for the GitHub secret CHECK_ACCOUNTS together with staging's viewing password). It's no longer copied over every week: once **Keep staging caught up** is on (System Configuration → Staging), live's changes come into staging within a minute and what you do on staging is kept; **Start staging over** is a button for when a fresh copy is wanted, and says first what staging would lose. On staging, every email, text, payment and outside call is held, sandboxed or paced (see *What does staging hold, and where do I see it?*), email is readable on its own Staging page, payments use the sandboxes, paid AI and the crawler stay off unless their switch is on (**Switches → Staging: paid AI**, **Staging: the crawler**), and staff sign in there with the test staff logins. The trvgo.us router's copy answers at staging.trvgo.us/go/, so the word "staging" is kept: no GO Code or shop can take it. A fresh copy (Start staging over) goes a table at a time and carries on by itself on the server, so the page can be closed: its bar shows how far it's got, and whoever started it gets an email when it's done (or if it stops). **Carry on starting over** nudges one that seems stuck. If **Open staging** ever meets a password prompt instead of letting you in, **Check the way in** (on the same page, Founder) asks staging for a page several ways (with nothing, with the pass, with the checks' sign-in, and the whole Open staging path) and says which part answered each: the web server, staging's guard or the page itself. It also shows the rules and settings on the way to staging's pages, never a password or key, with a **Copy the answers** button to send them to whoever is fixing it. Its first run found why Open staging kept asking: the rule that keeps staging's private folder shut also shut its pages, so its self-test put the browser's password prompt back. That's fixed, and Open staging lets you straight in.

### I'm on the Traversence team. I pressed Keep staging caught up: why doesn't it say on yet?
Turning it on starts staging over once, so both sides match before anything comes in from live. While that fresh copy goes in, **System Configuration → Staging** says **Keep staging caught up: turning on**, with how far the copy has got (its percentage and step), and the Now line says *turning on*. It switches on by itself at the copy's last step, and you get an email; then it reads **(on)** with **Stop keeping caught up**. Until then there's no button to press again: **Don't turn it on** cancels only the switch-on (the copy carries on, and nothing is added to live). While a copy is going in, or if one stopped part way, staging holds half a copy, so **Set up staging**, **Preview**, **Publish** and **Schedule** wait, each saying why; if the copy stopped, the page says where, with **Try again** or **Leave it off**.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How does a change reach the site?
Through **System Configuration → Releases**, or the **site manager** at the top of staging. A release is a zip of the changed files. Uploaded on either (the site manager's **Upload**), it goes straight onto staging.trvgo.us: its plan comes from the zip's release.json (what it changes, its switch), its database updates run there and it's checked: its code reads cleanly, its database updates only add, its pages answer and the error logs stay quiet. Then someone looks at it on staging (opening it there counts as the look), and **Publish** is the sign-off: it puts it live at once, or **Schedule** at a time you choose. A release with a switch goes to staff and test accounts first, then a share of members (25% unless changed), then everyone, each step on its own after its hours pass clean (24, then 72, on weekdays 6 am to 6 pm Arizona time) or when the next step is published. A failed check pauses it and emails whoever approved it. **Turn off** switches it off for everyone at once; **Roll back** puts the previous copy of every file back. An urgent fix can be published straight away with a reason, and is checked straight after. To try it yourself, **Open staging** (on System Configuration → Staging or Releases) or **Open on staging** (on a release) takes you there past staging's viewing password, signed in as you (or as one of the audit's test accounts, to see it as they would), with the **site manager** at the top of every page, in the admin's command bar look: **Releases ▾** (what's on staging now, what's waiting, what's live and at which step), **Upload** (your device's own file picker: a release zip isn't kept in the Gallery), **Changes** (the pages a release touches, to jump to, its files and checks), **Note** (pins what you see on that page to the release) and **Publish ▾** (Publish now, Schedule, the next step, then Turn off and Roll back once it's live), which act on the live site as you. ◆ folds it to a small tab. Publish copies exactly the files that were tried on staging, and stops if one of them changed on the live site since the release was built. Each release is recorded in git, shows in Pulse while it waits on someone, and its zip and rollback zip can be downloaded for an upload by FTP instead. Who may publish, schedule, turn off and roll back is set in **System Configuration → Permissions** (the Founder only, until a role is given them). Claude's finished builds arrive on staging by themselves from git (the site looks every 10 minutes, or **Releases ▾ → Check for builds** at once), so there's nothing to upload. Each is approved in the chat before it's sent, so once its checks on staging pass it goes live by itself; one that failed a check stops on staging and shows in Pulse. Releases can stack on staging (a file a later release changes again shows *replaced by #57*). A zip you upload on staging yourself waits there and goes live with your own work on staging, by **Publish** in the site manager (see *How does Publish carry what I did on staging?*). One the Founder asked to see first is **sent for review**: an email and a Pulse case take him onto staging, where the site manager's **Review** asks for **UI** (how it looks), **UX** (how it works for a person) and **Functional** (it does what it should), each **Approve** or **Needs changes** with a note kept with the release; **Publish** opens once all three are approved. The site manager keeps itself current (every minute, and when you come back to the tab); **Releases ▾ → Check for builds** fetches new builds at once. **Turn off** and **Roll back** work as for any release. Publish is for work done on staging itself: a zip uploaded there, and the page editor that's coming. Every change to the site's files should come through a release: one made straight on the server or in OneDrive shows in **Pulse → Site health** as *changed without a release*, with the files, and clears by itself once a release carries it.


### I'm on the Traversence team. Do I need to reload the Releases page to see where a release stands?
No. **System Configuration → Releases** lists the releases as a table: a header row (Release, Where it stands, When, Files), then one row per release, its title starting with its mark (a green check when its checks passed or it's live, a red X when a check failed, it withdrew itself or it's paused, a grey dash when it hasn't been checked yet or a person withdrew it). Above it are a search box and one **Shown** filter (waiting, live, a check failed, withdrawn, rolled back or off). A release opens on its toggle, under its title. While the page is open it looks again every 15 seconds, and at once when you come back to its tab: each release that moved on is drawn again where it is, and a new one appears at the top. What you opened stays open, and a row you're typing a note in waits until you're done. A release's checks are one line that opens to the list, folded unless a failed check is waiting on you, and a release shows a notice only when there's more to say than its mark: a failed check, a pause, or a step waiting on someone. A press answers in place, without a reload. On a phone each row stacks.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What happens when a build from Claude fails its checks on staging?
It withdraws itself: the moment its checks fail, or within 10 minutes at the next look. You don't press anything. Nothing on the live site changes, because a build goes live only once its checks pass. Staging is put back as it was: the build's database updates leave staging's folder (a failed one would hold the ones after it), and each of its files gets the live site's copy back. A file that a later build on staging also changes keeps that build's copy. The release's record says which check failed and why, so the Claude session that built it sends a fixed build, which names the one it replaces. A zip a person uploaded stays on staging for that person to fix or withdraw. **Withdraw** on **System Configuration → Releases** puts staging back the same way.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What happens to the database when a release goes out?
A release's database updates (its migrations) run as the first step of **Publish**, before any of its files go live, after they've already run on staging. If one fails, nothing is published: the error shows where you pressed Publish, in the release's record and in Pulse, and **Publish** again tries once the cause is fixed (or a fixed release replaces it). Updates still waiting on the server run within the hour as a backup. Just before each update runs, a compressed copy of the tables it touches is kept for 14 days on **System Configuration → Database updates → Copies before migrations**, each with **Restore**, which puts those tables' rows back as they were (a copy of how they are just then is kept first, so a restore can be undone too; who may restore is set in **System Configuration → Permissions**, Executives until a role is given it). A table too large to copy safely on our hosting is skipped, and the list says why; an update that changes or removes data never runs without its copy, and one of those also needs its reason written in the release. Updates only add, so **Roll back** leaves what a release's updates made (new tables, columns, indexes) where it is: that's listed on Database updates as *left by release #N*, and if the release isn't sent again within 30 days, removing it is proposed there and in a Pulse case, a step for each, for the Founder to approve. Once a month the site also lists, in one Pulse case, the tables and columns none of its code mentions, for the Founder to decide on. They're also listed on **System Configuration → Database updates** under **Unused tables and columns** (https://traversence.com/admin/config/dbupdates#unused), each with its database and size. There is nothing to do there: Claude checks each one and brings the Founder one list to approve (safe to remove, keep and why, unsure); nothing is removed without that OK, and a copy is kept first. A removal goes out as a release of its own, with a copy taken first. **Keep it** leaves one where it is.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do the admin's lists work?
Every list in the admin is the same table. The count is always above the page bar ("1–50 of 1,234"; after a search or filter, how many match), with **Show 10, 25, 50 or 100** a page (remembered on that device), the page bar, **Show more** under the list, and **⚙ Columns** to choose which show. Tap a column's name to sort by it, again to reverse. A row's coloured dot always comes with its word (Working, Held, Tripped). Times are where they happened, with their time zone. A row may have one button for its main action and **⋯** for the rest, each with its words; a greyed one says why you can't use it. A group (an outside service's alarms, say) is one row with its count: tap it to open its members under it. A column with nothing in it yet is named under the table ("Last 24 hours, Speed: no history yet") rather than hidden. On a phone each row is a card. The smaller tables the admin's pages write themselves (the Automation, Communications, Crawler, Operations and Indexer consoles, and the lists on Insights, Sources, Missed searches, Published, a content template's Versions and a guide's names) follow the same look: tap a column's name to sort it (again to reverse, a third time for the page's own order), numbers line up on the right, a column with nothing in it yet is named under the table, on a phone each row is a card, and past 25 rows the count, **Show 10, 25, 50 or 100** and the pages appear. A link to one row (a source from its service card) opens the page that row is on. A list that reads only its newest few hundred (orders, alarms, codes…) says so: "1–50 of 400+", with a line to search or filter to reach the rest.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do I see which files are being worked on?
In the **site manager** at the top of staging, **Releases ▾ → Checked out** lists each file a Claude session is writing a release for: the file (and the functions, when it changes existing ones), whether it adds or changes, which session, why and since when. One near its 4-hour expiry is marked ⚠, and tapping one opens that session. **Pulse → Site health** shows the same list as a step. Adding to a file is shared, so several sessions can add to one file at once. A change holds only the functions it names, and only until its release is live. A claim ends by itself when its release is live, or after 4 hours without a release. Nothing here locks you out of the site.

### What does staging hold, and where do I see it?
Everything that would leave the site from staging stays there, and is listed: in the **site manager** at the top of staging, **Staging ▾ → Held**, and on the live admin's **System Configuration → Staging**. Email (sign-in links, notices, work orders, Pulse) is held and readable there, so a link in it can still be followed on staging; **Send to me** sends one to your own address, marked as from staging, never to the person it was for. Payments use the sandboxes only, and stop altogether if a live payment key is ever in staging's settings (the site manager says which). Paid services and AI (Anthropic, Gemini, Google, openrouteservice, Pixabay and the rest) are held; one can be allowed for a test for a while, counted against live's limits. Free public data (OpenStreetMap, Wikipedia, the Weather Service, the Census) is read, paced well inside live's limits, since staging shares live's address, and bulk pulls are held. Domains are never connected from staging, social sign-in is off, nothing there is indexed, and every page carries a policy that lets only what our pages need load: anything else is blocked and listed in Held. When something you did was held, the notice beside the button says so, starting **TEST MODE: ACTIVE (STAGE REPLIES ONLY)**, then which service wasn't called and why (held, sandbox only, or paced), and "Nothing was sent." Every outside call the site makes asks staging first, the same way: sign-in checks, the hosting account (cPanel), Amazon's email and Textract, GitHub, Railway, payments, photos and the public data reads; only the file store and calls to staging itself go straight through. Staging also runs its own copies of the scheduled jobs once **Staging runs its own jobs** is on (jobs that only talk to the outside world never run there); each of staging's jobs starts 7 minutes after live's (2026-11-26), so the two never crowd the server in the same minute. Push notifications are held the same way, and connected mailboxes are never read or sent from on staging. Communications' setup never changes a mailbox, its password, the site's settings file or Amazon's email account from staging: it says it's staging and stops. (Until 2026-11-19 that check could answer "live" on staging by mistake; it now asks the same check as everything else.) Coming: texts, held the same way once anything sends them.

### How does Publish carry what I did on staging?
Publish is for the work you do on staging itself; Claude's builds go live on their own. **Publish ▾ → Preview** (in the site manager, or System Configuration → Staging) works out what changes on live: any zip you uploaded on staging, and the pages, listings, settings, workflows and other records made, changed or removed there, counted by kind, each row on a click, with what isn't carried and why, what's held, and how long it should take. A field both sides changed differently is a **conflict**: shown side by side (as they last matched, staging's, live's) for you to choose **Keep live** or **Use staging** (**Keep live for the rest** settles the others); a field only one side changed just takes that side's, so nothing is lost. **Publish** (asked once) then runs on the server, a step at a time, while live stays open: the uploaded zips' database updates, then staging's data a batch at a time (each row only if live's still matches the preview; one changed meanwhile is kept as live has it and listed), then the uploaded zips' files and staging's uploaded files, then the checks on live. You can close the page; an email says when it's done or where it stopped. A row made on staging takes its number from live, and every link to it moves with it. What's never carried: test accounts and what they made, payments and orders (all sandbox there), the stand-ins for people's details, sessions and settings of staging itself, and staging's own views and counts. Held emails are never sent by Publish: afterwards **To send now** offers each, to send from live or drop.
