You'll see its mandate, the steps it has, what it acts as, the highest tier it may decide alone (Tier 3 always goes to the Founder), the decisions it works under and its **History**. **✎ Edit its words** changes its name, mandate, what it works on and its rules, and the old words are kept in its History. **Put on standby**, **Retire it** and **Make active** each have their way back on the same card, and a role is never deleted. What a role may do comes only from Permissions, for the level and department it acts as, never from the Team page. A role with no steps yet does no work until one is written for it. The agents work only on the staging copy (see "What does the Team's runner do?"); nothing they do reaches the live site until staging is published in a batch the Founder approved.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What does the Team's runner do?
On the staging copy only, and only while its switch is on (System Configuration → Switches there), every ten minutes it takes one ticket from the Work queue and does one step for one working role, then stops. How far it goes is set by **Agents work up to** on the Team page, which stays at **Planning** until the Founder raises it (see "What is the Team's decision tree?"). At Planning, the **Project Manager** proposes moving a Work ticket to Building once its gate passes, and a person's **Accept** makes the move. Above Planning it makes the move itself. The **System Architect** writes a Building ticket's build brief for the session manager, but only once the ceiling is above Planning. The **Executive Translator** proposes the plain words of a Decision Brief from what we hold. It never works a ticket a person has (only unowned ones, or ones a Team role has), never answers a Tier 3 decision (only the Founder does), never writes over words a person wrote (it proposes them instead, and a person's **Accept** fills them in, kept in the History), and never calls an outside service or paid AI. Each step is a line on the ticket's page: who did it, what and when, with **Undo** for a move or a brief (put back as if it never happened; a second Undo is refused) and **Accept** or **Dismiss** for a proposal. What it can do comes only from Permissions for the role's level and department; a step it isn't allowed says so and changes nothing. On the live site it does nothing at all. What it did on staging reaches the live site only when staging is published, in the batch the Founder approved.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What is the before-and-after check?
It's on (switched on with the fence on Nov 26, 2026, and running for every release from Claude since the next release after that): each one waits on staging for one more check before it can go live (decisions/0179). If the check's switch (**Switches → The before-and-after check**) is turned off, releases go on without it, as they did before. The advocates look at the pages the release touches, each as its own test account, and the Staff Views advocate as the staff test logins. They look at 1280 and 390 wide, once before the release goes on staging and again after. If any view is worse afterwards, the release stops. Worse means one of four things: a page opens for an account that shouldn't see it, or shows someone else's details or a protected place (decisions/0066); the layout breaks, a button goes missing, or the page scrolls sideways on a phone; rows or cards go missing; or a page changes that the release doesn't mention. A stopped release withdraws itself, nothing on the live site changes, and the problem goes in the Work queue with a link to the report. A release that passes goes on as before. If the check's daily time on GitHub has run out, or it couldn't sign the test accounts in, the release waits for a person instead of going live by itself. For protected places, the check reads the names the site holds back from staging only as coded fingerprints, never the names themselves, and a release that makes one of them appear on a page stops; if they can't be read, the release waits for a person. You'll see it in a release's checks on System Configuration → Releases, as **Before and after (advocates)**.


### I'm on the Traversence team. Which releases can go live without the daily batch?
Coming, and off until the Founder turns it on (decisions/0179 rule 2). Today, everything Claude builds waits for the Founder to approve the daily batch. With rule 2 on, a release goes live by itself once staging's checks and the advocates pass it (see "What is the before-and-after check?"), unless it touches money, data, public addresses, tribal nations' land or privacy. Those are Tier 3: they never go live by themselves, and each comes to the Founder as a decision in the Work queue. A data change counts as Tier 3 when its database update changes, deletes, drops, modifies or renames something; one that only adds a new table, column or index doesn't. Data fixes (updates that write rows) still wait for the batch. A release that says it's a low-risk kind (a FAQ change, a wording change, a phone-layout tweak or a safe additive change) is also proved by comparing each file with the live copy, and is marked Tier 1. Each release that went live this way shows under **Published alone today** at the top of the Work queue (System Control Board → **Development**), marked Tier 1 with its kind or Tier 2, with **Undo**, which rolls it back.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How does the Founder hear about the daily batch and decisions?
The site emails the Founder itself, through its own system email, so nothing outside has to be allowed to send mail. Three things are sent, each once. Today's batch of releases waiting for approval goes out when the session manager writes it, with the link to approve it. A Tier 3 decision goes out when its brief is sent and it's waiting for an answer, with the options and the link to its page. Messages from the session manager go out as it writes them. Each also lands in the Founder's Pulse. Only the Founder gets them, and the staging copy never sends them.
### I'm on the Traversence team. What is the Team's decision tree?
It's at the top of System Configuration → **Team**. In plain words, it shows what the Team may do alone and what always comes to the Founder: the three tiers, the areas that make anything Tier 3 (money, data, public addresses, tribal nations' land, privacy), and each working role's step at each stage. It's drawn from the runner's own rules, so it always says what the code does. The Founder presses **Approve the decision tree**, and the approval is tied to a fingerprint of the rules. Changing a word of explanation keeps it. Changing any rule clears it, puts agents back at Planning, and says so in the History. Under the tree is **Agents work up to**, which is Planning to begin with. Only the Founder can raise it, one stage at a time, and only once the tree is approved. Each raise shows its evidence (what agents have done so far and how much of it people accepted or undid), with where each reading comes from, and the Founder judges it. There are no pass marks. **Lower** puts it back at any time. At **Building**, the Project Manager moves tickets on by itself, and takes the tickets the audit raised itself; a ticket a person entered still waits for **Give it to the Team**. At **Implementation**, the System Architect's build brief also goes straight to a build session. **Confirmation** opens once the before-and-after check and rule 2 are both switched on: there, agents act as at Implementation, and a release that touches no Tier 3 area goes live by itself once staging's checks and the advocates pass it. Every agent step is logged with its Undo; one that couldn't be undone is refused and shows. Each role may spend $0 a month, since no step calls paid AI. In the Work queue, a ticket the agents found says **Found by the audit**, a ticket with proposals says how many, and the filter has **Found by agents** and **Has suggestions**. Agents never take a ticket themselves: a person gives one to them with **Give it to the Team** in its ⋯.


### I'm on the Traversence team. Can the agents change pages people see?
Not for now. Under the decision tree on System Configuration → **Team** is **How far Implementation reaches**, set to **Backend only**, and only the Founder can change it. Once the agents work at Implementation, a ticket whose change stays in the backend (the admin's pages, the site's internal code and the workers) gets its build brief sent to a build session. A ticket whose change would reach a page a visitor, member or business sees stops at its brief: its page in the Work queue says **Front end: waiting for you** and names the file that reaches the public, and the Founder decides. Which is which comes from a map of what each file reaches, made from the live site after every sync. A ticket that names no file can't be shown to stay in the backend, so it waits too. When a build arrives, it's checked again: one that reaches a public page is refused, nothing is sent, and its ticket goes back to its brief. Everything the agents build still waits for the daily batch until the before-and-after check is on. Changing the setting changes the decision tree's rules, so the tree has to be approved again.
### I'm on the Traversence team. What is the Team's daily audit?
Once a day on the staging copy, while the Team's runner is switched on, the **Operations & Reconciliation Manager** reads what we already know: Site health's open findings, scheduled jobs that are late or failed, and open alarms. Each new finding becomes one **Audit** ticket in the Work queue, at **Found**. The next day it's looked at again. If it's gone, it's cleared by its own check as noise and nobody is asked anything. If it's still there, it's **Confirmed** and opens the ticket it calls for. That's a **Work** ticket for an ordinary fix, or a **Resolution** for a decision only the Founder makes: money, deleting or reshaping data, public addresses, tribal nations' land and sensitive sites, or privacy (decisions/0179). What makes a finding Tier 3 is what it touches: a page, file, scheduled job, outside service, library or table in one of those areas. Words alone never do. A finding only worded like one of them (a "refund" note on a trail page) stays Tier 2, and its row says **Words suggest a Tier 3 boundary: a person checks**. On its page, **It's Tier 2** clears that note, and **It's Tier 3** makes it a decision for the Founder (for a Work ticket, its Resolution opens at once). Either way it's kept in the History. The filter's **Words suggest Tier 3** shows them all. The mission and a deadlock between decisions have no pages or files of their own, so a finding about either is always this kind of note for a person, never decided by the Team. The Audit ticket closes once that ticket is done. Each ticket the audit opens can be undone (withdrawn) from its page. **Run the audit now**, on the role's card in System Configuration → Team, runs it at once on staging.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What is Today's batch at the top of the Work queue?
Until the site can check a release's before and after by itself, nothing that needs approval goes live until the Founder approves the day's batch (decisions/0179). Once a day the session manager writes it. Each line is one release waiting: what it does, why, its decision, the checks it passed on staging, and its way back. It appears at the top of the Work queue (System Control Board → **Development**) on its own day, and after that as **The latest batch** until it's approved or a newer one replaces it, with any Tier 3 decisions waiting for the Founder listed first. **Approve the batch** is the Founder's alone, signed in with the Founder's own staff login. For everyone else it's greyed, saying why. The approval reaches the session manager within the hour. The Founder can also answer by replying to the daily summary's email. Each release in it that has gone live is marked **Live as #** with its number, and once every one has (the Founder can also approve in chat), the line says **Every release in it is live** and asks nothing more. When there's none waiting, the line says **No batch from the session manager today**. A Tier 3 decision the Founder answers by email is put on its ticket by the next read, as "Jason (by email, recorded by the session manager)", kept in the History. It's put on only once, and only while the ticket is still waiting for an answer.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What does "Paused by the system" mean?
It's a trip: something failed, the system tried again by itself and couldn't fix it, so it placed a pause on what the fault touches, and only that (one task, one workflow, calls to one service). It never turns a switch off: every switch stays as the person who set it left it, and the pause shows beside it. It lifts by itself when its check passes (the alarm closes as **Recovered**), or at once with **Check and start again**, which checks first. Something a person switched off reads **Off**, with who and why, and is never called paused by the system.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What does Recovered mean on an alarm?
The system fixed it by itself: its check passed again, what it paused runs again, and the alarm closed. Its message is the same one as the trip, closed, saying how long the part was down ("Recovered: W1 Listings was down 12 min, the system fixed it"). There's nothing to press. If a person's **Check and start again** closed it, it reads **Fixed by** their name instead.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What is an Incident Report (IR-####)?
Every trip opens one, or joins an open one with the same cause (the same host and kind) within 15 minutes. The **Incidents** view on the System Control Board lists them; each opens its page: the parts in it (each warning W-#### and trip T-####), what it held, its timeline and its evidence. Its root cause reads **Unconfirmed** until a person writes it: **Write the root cause** asks for it in words and keeps it on the report with your name (and in the logbook); **Change the root cause** edits it. Your role may not allow it; then the button is greyed with the reason.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do I copy part of the System Control Board?
Every section and card on the board, its reports and its gauges' panels has a **Copy** button: it puts what that part shows on the clipboard as plain text (its headings and rows, a table's cells separated by tabs), never what's folded away or hidden. The answer shows beside the button. A whole card made of sections (a service's card in the Service register) has **Copy all** on its first section's heading, beside that section's own **Copy**: Copy all takes the whole card, Copy only that section (2026-11-23).

### I'm on the Traversence team. What happens if the whole site is down?
A check outside our server (on GitHub, every 20 minutes) opens the home page and asks the site whether its databases and its watch are working. If not, it emails the Founder at once, one message while it's down, and a reply in the same thread when it's back. When the site is up again, what the check saw is added to the board as a trip with its real times and its Incident Report, and nothing is sent twice. It's built and waits for its settings (where to send it, and its own send-only email key); until they're added it sends nothing (coming).

### I'm on the Traversence team. Where did Site health go?
It's on the System Control Board's **Development** view, as its own list: each finding of the site audit with the page, who it blocks and how badly, and its buttons. Each new finding also reaches Pulse once, as its own item, never as a push. A site audit that hasn't run on time is a warning on the board, with the link to run it.
### I'm on the Traversence team. How am I told about a visitor's photo of a place, or new artwork on an order?
Since 2026-11-20 (decisions/0176 release 4), each is one thread in your Pulse, updated in place, never sent twice:
- **A photo of a place** (if you see Reports): "New photo of <the place>", or "New photos of <the place> (3)", when its journey goes live (it's published, or a photo is added to a published one). A place's photo is live at once (post first). Inside it: **Open it** (the journey, in the side panel) and **Hide**, with a reason its author sees; then **Show again** puts it back. A report on it joins the same thread: while it's open, the report's own case in Pulse is that one row, with its clock, Take it, Assign and Escalate as before, and the photo's steps inside it: see the photos, **Take it down**, **Leave it up**, or **Hide** with your own reason. Once it's decided, the photo's thread is its own row again.
- **New artwork on an order** (if you work on orders; never the person who added the file): "New artwork on Order #N", with **Open the order**, which opens its Files in the side panel, where its proofs and approval are. **Ask for a new file** and **Check the files** are greyed: the file check (resolution, size and bleed) comes once Jason sets its limits.
- Neither ever comes by push. Email is off unless you switch it on in **Dashboard → Your communications** (https://traversence.com/admin/dashboard/comms).

### I'm on the Traversence team. Am I told when a Pulse case is given to me?
Yes. When a teammate assigns you a case, or a case in your department is escalated to you as its Manager or Director, you get an email (to your staff mailbox) naming the case, and it's at the top of your Pulse stream. With no Manager or Director set for the department, an escalation reaches the Founder. Your own choices for these, by email or push, and your quiet hours, are in **Dashboard → Your communications** (https://traversence.com/admin/dashboard/comms).

### I'm on the Traversence team. Can I read support@ or my own email in Pulse?
Yes, in **Dashboard → My inbox** (https://traversence.com/admin/dashboard/inbox): the mail of every mailbox you read, together, each row labelled with its mailbox, one row per conversation. A row opens in place with the email shown safely (outside pictures wait for **Show pictures**), and its answer box: Reply, Reply all or Forward, **From** any mailbox you read, and Send; a reply goes from that mailbox and lands in its own Sent folder. Mark unread, Archive, Junk and **Make it a case** are on the opened row. An owner chooses who reads support@, orders@ and the rest in Communications; your own staff mailbox is always yours. You can also connect an outside mailbox of yours (IMAP, POP, Gmail with an app password, or Sign in with Microsoft) once your manager approves it. Sign in with Google is coming, once revenue covers Google's yearly security check. The same mail is also in the Pulse stream itself (https://traversence.com/admin/dashboard/stream#mail): each email a row among everything else, **Mail** to show only them, and on its row the arrow forwards it, the shield moves it to Junk, blocks the sender (for you alone; they aren't told) or reports phishing to the team, and the plus makes it a case or a task or adds the sender to the Address Book.
### I'm on the Traversence team. How do I work the Pulse stream?
Each row is one thing, from whoever sent it: a case that used to bundle many things (the engine's "needs a person", Site health's findings) now shows one row per thing. Tap a row (or its ▾) to open it in place, under itself: what happened, where it stands, its steps with their buttons (Mark shipped, Answer it, Fixed or fine…), the latest notes and a box for a note; **Work on it** opens the wider case drawer with the tool beside it. Each row's icons: the envelope for read or unread, the arrow to pass it on (assign to a teammate), the pin to pin it in your own list, the shield to escalate, the folder to file it or Archive it, the plus in a circle for a Follow up, importance and tags (your own, or the team's), and the down arrow to open it. The stream is a table, one line per message like a mail inbox: its level first (an icon; hold or hover for its name), who it's from, the subject with its words after it, and when it came in (under the tray icon: the date, or the time for today; point at it for the whole moment with its time zone). On a phone the level icon comes first, before the sender's. The stream shows the newest first. Tap ! to put the most important first (overdue rows, then by level), again for the least important first, a third time for the newest first again. Only work someone owes an answer to has a time to be handled: support, sales, orders, payments, safety and security (System, Site health and Control Board items have none; a Control Board trip nobody resolves still goes to the Manager, then the Director). The bell lists what's critical, overdue, or escalated to you. A row past its time to be handled is tinted amber (red when it's Critical) and shows a clock in place of its level icon, amber, red when it's Critical; point at it for its level and how far over. The bell lists those waiting on you. Each row ends with **▾** (open it here) and **⋯**, a menu that names each action (a greyed one says why); nothing pops over the text. Tap a column's name to sort by it (again to reverse, a third time for the stream's own order), and drag a column's edge to make it wider or narrower; your choices are remembered on that device.  The Operational Task Queue is gone: the bell beside the chips shows what's critical or escalated to you, and At the top, one line: **Filter this list**, the bell, **Your day** (the calendar: it opens wide over the page; ×, a tap outside or Esc closes it), **⋯** for Quick actions, and the stream's settings (the sliders), which open in a panel over Pulse; on a wide screen each says its name beside its icon. Under it, one button shows which list and view you're looking at ("Inbox · All (24)"): tap it for your lists (Inbox, Pinned, Follow up, Archived, your folders), **Show** (**Mine**, given to you; **Resolved**, closed; and the stream's other views), **Senders** and **Levels** (each with how many your other choices leave, a greyed 0 for none; untick the ones you don't want), **Group by**, and **Saved views**. Each choice applies at once and the panel stays open, so you can combine them; what's on shows as tags beside the button, each with × to take it off (**Clear all** for every one). Name a mix under **Saved views** ("Needs me now") and it comes back with one tap on any device you sign in on; × removes one, with Undo; **+ New** lists what you can start: **Post to the team** (everyone or one department), **A task** (for you or a teammate, with a day; it comes back then), **A client order** and **A listing** (each opens beside Pulse). **An email** from a mailbox you read and **A message to one teammate** are coming. Every pop-up closes with its **×**, Esc or a tap outside it. Each column heading shows its sort arrow (faint until you sort by it; tap again to turn it round). Under the list: how many there are ("1–50 of 312", "12 of 312 match" while filtering), **Show 10, 25, 50 or 100** a page (remembered), and the pages; a new filter or sort starts at page 1. **Group by** gathers the rows under one heading each, by **Sender**, **Kind** (Support, Fulfillment, System…) or **Level**, with how many; a group of 3 rows or fewer opens by itself, a tap on a heading opens or closes it (remembered on that device), **Open all** and **Close all** sit beside the filter button, and **None** is the plain list. It's yours, remembered on that device. **Inbox**, **Pinned**, **Follow up**, **Archived** and your folders above the stream show each set; your marks are yours alone. Messages the site sends to you (a case given to you, an email that failed, a follow-up coming back) and the mail you read are rows too, opening in place; a tap on one in the bell opens its row here. Signed in with your staff login, the bell at the top of every page counts the same things as Pulse's own bell (critical, overdue and escalated to you) and opens Pulse with them showing; your staff login has no member dashboard, so your password and sign-in code are at https://traversence.com/admin/staff.php and your own member account is one tap away in the account switch at the top left. Related system alarms show as one row that opens to each alarm. In a case's drawer, **Previous** and **Next** move through the list as you've sorted and filtered it, and the plus on any row adds **A task…** for you or a teammate, with the day to follow up.


### I'm on the Traversence team. Where is the site's email set up?
In **System Configuration → Communications** (https://traversence.com/admin/config/communications): each sender (the system sender, orders@, requests@ and Amazon SES) with **Check** (signs in without sending and says exactly what's wrong) and **Send me a test**; every mailbox on every one of our domains, to make, suspend, unsuspend or reset (a password you type, or one the site makes and writes into its settings itself, never shown); who reads each one in Pulse; which kind of email goes from which sender; SPF, DKIM and DMARC for our domains with a fix button; and message control: what's paused and why, the last week by channel, and everything sent. Viewing is for Managers and up; changes are for the owner.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do I read the support email?
In the admin, **Pulse → Support inbox**, then **Open the support inbox**. It opens support@traversence.com's webmail in a new tab, already signed in, so there's no mailbox password to type or share. The Founder, executives and managers in Service can open it; others can be given it in System Configuration → Permissions ("Open the support inbox"). Each time it's opened, who opened it is noted.

### I'm on the Traversence team. Where is everything in the admin?
The admin has 10 modules, in the menu on the left (☰ on a phone). Each module has its own sections, listed in the menu next to the page title. Your role and department decide which modules you see. Anything your role can't do is greyed out, with the reason. A section that's coming is greyed out in that menu and opens a page saying what it will do. Lists work the same way everywhere: a search box, filters, and the stage of each item. Tap an item to open it in a panel at the side. The panel shows where the item stands, how long it's been there, and the next steps you can take. The round icons beside the section menu open tools from other modules in a panel over your work, so you don't lose your place, and Back closes the panel. Managers and up can change the steps work goes through, and who may take each one, in **System Configuration → Workflows**. The older admin pages still open from their old links and the site map, with a note saying where their work is now. To find anything, use the admin's search (Ctrl K, or ⌘K on a Mac): it finds pages and tools, records (orders, listings, guides, people and more), things you can start, help answers and our decisions, and each scheduled task and switch by its name or what it does ("audit clock" finds the Site audit clock in both **Scheduled tasks** and **Switches**), opening its card on its page.


### I'm on the Traversence team. Where are Development, the Board, Workflows, Services and the Team?
In the side menu, under **System Configuration**: while you're in System Configuration, its five main pages are listed there, Development (the Work queue), Board (the System Control Board), Workflows, Services (the Service register) and Team. The one you're on is marked, and one your role can't open is greyed, saying why. On a phone they're in the ☰ menu the same way. The section list at the top of the page still has every section.

### I'm on the Traversence team. What's in the bar at the top of each admin page?
One dark row joined to the site's header, staying put as you scroll. On the left: **Back**, the module's name, its **sections** menu (▾), and the page's own filter when it has one. On the right: the System Control Board's light (amber for a warning, red only for a trip; not on the Dashboard, where Pulse lists the trips), the tools **At hand** (each opens in a drawer over your work) and **Shortcuts**. On a phone it takes two rows. To search anything in the admin, use the search box in the site's header (Ctrl K, or ⌘K on a Mac). A list's own box filters only that list. Red is kept for something that has stopped the system; amber means a routine hold or timeout the system is retrying.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do I get from a scheduled task to its page, and back?
In **System Configuration → Scheduled tasks**, each task that looks after a page has an **Open** button naming it (the Pre-flight task has **Open Pre-flight**). Each of those pages shows a slim line near the top: when the task runs, when it last ran (in your time, labelled) and **Change**, which opens that task's card on Scheduled tasks. Changing schedules needs that permission (directors in Engineering or Operations, by default); anyone without it sees Change greyed out, with the reason. A task that stopped, is late or isn't set up shows that line as a red notice instead. A task that only runs when it has something to do (Start the system's, carrying on a start you pressed; staging's copies; releases) is never called late: its line says it has had nothing to do since its last run (2026-11-20). A few tasks have no page of their own (files to S3, the weekly notices email, likes and guests' 30 days, earned storage, the sign-up region check, the site audit clock), so they have no Open button.

### I'm on the Traversence team. How do I test the Marketplace?
Post with **Test post** ticked (it's ticked for team accounts; untick it for a real post). A test post shows only to our team, marked **Test**, and never to anyone else. With a second team account (or a teammate) as the buyer, you can message, make and counter offers, tap Sold and Got it, leave a note and report a problem, all as usual. Tests use up no one's 5 posts and count in no numbers, notes or trust points. Find them in Market 360° → Items under **Test posts**, and take them down when you're done.
### What does Peek do in search?
Tapping a result opens its page. Every result also has a **Peek** button: on a listing or an outdoor place it opens a quick card with the address, Call and Directions; on anything else (a story, a journey) it opens the page in a window over the one you're on. Either way you don't leave the page you're on. The page dims behind the card; tap outside it, ×, or press Esc to close it.

### How do I add a photo of a place?
Tap the camera button at the bottom right of the photo at the top of a place or a business. It starts a journey with the place already filled in: add your photos or video and a few words, and publish. Once it's published, your photo shows at the top of that page, credited to you. A business's own photos come first, and visitors' photos follow them. If you run the business, the camera is **Photos & video** instead: it chooses the page's own photos. Places on a tribal nation's land follow the nation's rules for photos and pins.

- **Is it live at once?** (2026-11-20) Yes, once its journey is published. When you add a photo, the answer beside the button says so: "it's live now" on a published journey, or "it goes live when you publish" on a draft. Our team sees each new photo of a place and can hide it if something's wrong; its author still sees it, with the reason. A report on it goes to the same people.

### Why do some names look different from other sites?
Some listings come from public data that shortens words, like "Visitor Ctr" or "Natl Pk". We show them in full ("Visitor Center", "National Park"). Once a business claims its listing, we show the name exactly as the owner writes it.

### Why does a listing say "Also known as"?
Some places go by more than one name: a new name after a change of owner, a legal name on official records, or an older name people still use. "Infinia at Show Low" and "Tall Pines Care and Rehab" are one nursing home, for example. The listing keeps its other names and shows them as **Also known as**, and searching any of them finds it. When our crawler finds the same place under another name (the same address, phone and kind of place), it adds what it learned to the existing listing and keeps the name, instead of listing it twice.

### Why does a listing say "Part of" another name?
Some organizations have several locations: a school district's schools, a health center's clinics, a chain's stores, an agency's offices. Each location has its own listing, with its own address, phone and hours, and says which organization it's **Part of**. Stores of a franchise brand show **Part of** the brand too, but each is its own business: its owner manages only that store's listing. Tap that name to see every location on one page, nearest you first when your place is set. Under each location's details, **Other locations** lists the nearest few, each with Call and Directions. Searching the organization's name finds all of its locations. Our team links them, from the organization's own website (its own list of its schools, clinics or stores) or records, never from a similar name alone. A chain's shared website never makes two of its stores one listing: each store is its own place. Owners claiming all their locations at once is coming.

### Why do several listings share one address and phone number?
Clinics, medical centers and offices often house several providers or businesses under one roof and one front-desk number: a behavioral health center and the counselors who work there, say. Each is its own listing, so you can find the person or service you need. When our crawler finds a business that is already listed under a shortened name ("Apache Behavioral Health Svc" and "Apache Behavioral Health Services"), with the same phone, street address and ZIP, it adds what it learned to the existing listing instead of making a second one, and never overwrites what's there. Our team can also link a facility and its providers, so the facility's page lists them (see "Part of" above).

### Can I see a place's photo bigger?
Yes. Tap the photo at the top of a place, a listing or a profile to open it full screen, with its credit. If there are several, swipe or use the arrows to move between them. The top of the photo says what it's of, with a link to its page, **See on the map** and **Copy link**; on Let's Explore, where the photos show different places, each one names its own. Under the photo are its views, likes and comments (zeros included): like it or go to its comments from there, and on its own page share it too. Tap outside the photo, or ×, to close it.

### What's under ⋯ at the foot of a page's photo?
The photo's corner keeps its numbers: views, likes and comments (zeros included). Everything else is under **⋯**, in words: first **Link to** (the page's own Link: follow it, and what's new shows in your Pulse), **Claim** (a business's or an organization's listing; greyed elsewhere, with why) and **Add to a trip**, then **Share** and **QR code**, **Share a photo or video** (or **Photos and video** for the page's owner), **Save to Address Book** on a business, then **See on the map**, **Plan a trip here** and **Report** where the page has them. While ⋯ is open, the page's Link icon steps aside so the two never sit on each other. What the page already shows beside its card (Call, Directions, the map, Save contact, its QR code, Suggest an edit) isn't repeated there. Something a page can't offer shows greyed, with the reason. Close it with ×, Esc or a tap outside it.

### How do I plan a trip to the place I'm looking at?
Tap ⋯ on the page's photo, then **Plan a trip here**. It shows the ready-made journeys for that area first (a day there, the outdoors, history, food: each timed stop by stop), then **Open the Trip Planner** already set to the place. Saving a trip of your own and adding places to it (**Add to a trip**) are coming.

### Why do some map spots show a number and "approx."?
They're listings whose exact spot isn't on the map yet. Most listings came to us with only their ZIP code, so they all sat on the middle of the ZIP area. Instead of fanning them out as if they were real places, the map shows one dashed circle with how many there are, like **157 approx.** Tap it to see them as a list, then tap one to open it. We place their street addresses on the map a batch at a time, every time our crawler runs, and each one moves to its real spot as soon as it's placed. Some addresses can't be placed that way, like PO boxes and rural routes, so those get their spot from the business's own website, from our team, or from you: open the listing and tap **Wrong spot? Fix the pin**.

### Why did my journey's pin move or disappear?
On a tribal nation's land, a pin shows only at a place the nation opens to visitors. If your pin is within half a mile of one of those places, it moves onto that place. Anywhere else on the nation's land the pin isn't shown, and your journey says where you were in your own words instead. This follows the nations' own rules: many areas are homes, grazing land or sacred places, and aren't open to visitors.

### Where do the visitor rules on a tribal place come from?
From the nation's own website. Our team checks each nation's rules for permits, fees, guides, photography, drones and alcohol, and the page says when they were last checked, with a link to the nation's visitor information. Rules change, so check that link before you go. If a nation asks us to close a place, we close it at once.
### I searched for something and got places from somewhere else. Why?
On Let's Explore, if there's nothing for your search in the place you're exploring, we look across the open regions and say so at the top of the results. Words for landscapes work too: try **badlands**, **canyon**, **petroglyphs**, **ruins** or **dark sky**.

### Why does search show different things on Let's Explore and Get Local?
Search reads where you are. On **Let's Explore** it puts the place's journeys, stories, outdoors, groups and topics first, with businesses after them. On **Get Local** businesses come first. The place you're searching sits at the top left (tap × to search everywhere), and **☆ Save** at the top right keeps the search. Tapping × on a place you typed (like "near Grants") searches the rest of your words everywhere.

### Why does search sometimes answer with a place's story?
When you ask about a place's past or a subject our guides cover ("St. Johns history", "Little Colorado River"), search answers first from what we've researched ourselves: a short summary and a few facts backed by historic records or two independent sources, with a link to the guide's "Walking in the footsteps". It says when we're still learning about something, and asking about it moves it up our research list. It never says where a sacred, burial or archaeological place is, and it says nothing about what a tribal nation or the law keeps private.

### Can I search the way I'd ask a friend?
Yes. Type it the way you'd say it: "new pizza place in town", "a good taco spot nearby", "pizza place in Show Low", "going fishing for apache trout", "where can we camp". When you name something to do, like fishing, hiking, camping or swimming, we look for places to do it, and anything else you mention ("apache trout") puts the places that mention it first. Search reads the place, the kind of business and the time ("open now"), and leaves out words that only describe it, like "new", "good" or "spot". "In town" and "nearby" mean the place you're searching. We can't yet tell which businesses opened recently, so "new" doesn't narrow the list; that's coming. "Where to buy new" reads as shopping, not as a search for the word "new".

Search reads each part of what you type for its job, even without a full sentence:
- **The thing you want**: "plumber", "hardware store", "oil change".
- **Words that narrow it**: "emergency", "urgent", "24 hour" or "after hours" put places open now first and leave out the ones known to be closed ("emergency plumber"). "Emergency room" is still a place, not a filter. Other words in front of the thing ("commercial plumber", "heavy duty generator") put the places that mention them first without hiding the rest.
- **Who it's for**: "for seniors", "for veterans"; "for beginners" looks for lessons and classes. "Family friendly restaurants" or "kid friendly pizza" puts the places that say so first.
- **Who's coming along**: "place to take my kids", "things to do with toddlers", "fun for teens", "what to do with a 5 year old", "where to take my dog" or "date night" shows the kinds of places that suit them (playgrounds, zoos, museums, splash pads and libraries for little ones; arcades, trampoline parks and go-karts for teens; dog parks and trails for the dog), nearest first and within about 25 miles. Buttons at the top switch between little ones, kids and teens, or indoors and outdoors. "Rainy day activities" or "things to do inside" shows indoor places.
- **Where**: "in", "near" and "around" mark the place, and so does a town at the end with no little word ("hardware store St Johns"). "St", "Mt" and "Ft" read as Saint, Mount and Fort.
- **What's going on**: phrases like "power went out" or "stuck on the highway" are situations (below).

### What are "Pairs with" and "Make a day of it" on a place's page?
**Pairs with** lists up to three other places within about 15 miles that go well with it, each a different kind (a canyon, a museum, a lake), the ones drawing the most interest first, with how far each is and what it's like. **Make a day of it** strings the place and two of those together in the order that drives least, ends with somewhere to eat nearby when there is one, gives the miles for each leg, and **Send to Maps** opens the whole route in Google Maps or Apple Maps. A place to eat from OpenStreetMap says so; it isn't a Traversence listing.

### Who writes what a place is like when Wikipedia has nothing?
When a place has no Wikipedia summary, Claude writes two or three sentences on what it is and what you do there, from what we hold and at most one web search, with its sources. It's kept with the place and reused, never written again. A Wikipedia summary itself is kept and shown only as far as Wikipedia's terms allow; otherwise the place links to its article. We write ahead for the 20 places in each town that draw the most interest, so most are ready before anyone opens them; others are written the first time someone opens them. Places that may be sacred or a nation's are never written about.

### Two towns have the same name. Which one does search pick?
The one nearest you, or nearest the place you've set, when it's within about 150 miles ("Glendale" in Phoenix is Glendale, AZ); otherwise the one in the states we cover, then the larger one. While typing, a town named exactly what you typed comes before longer names that start the same way ("Phoenix" before Phoenixville).

While you type, two letters that could be a state's code aren't assumed to be the state: "dog pa" offers **Dog parks** first and asks "“dog” in Pennsylvania?" as a choice. After Enter, or with a comma ("show low, az"), the code is read as the state. A state named in your words is the place, at the start or the end: "arizona food" and "food in arizona" both show food across Arizona, nearest to you first. A town's own name always wins ("Virginia Beach", "Arizona City").

### Can I just describe what's going on?
Yes. Type it the way you'd say it: "the power went out", "stuck on the highway", "lost my keys", "job interview coming up", "I can't pay my bills". Search recognises the situation and shows what it needs, each part on its own: for being stuck on the road, **Towing**, **Roadside repair**, **Gas** and **Highway patrol**, each in your place first and then nearest. When something is urgent, the phone number and **Call** come first, and places known to be open now lead. Everything else your words found is one tap away under **Everything else**. You don't have to use any particular words: search also reads what they mean. "Bit by something", "a dog bit me" and "got stung" are all an injury; "my sink is leaking" is a plumbing problem; "ran out of my meds" is about prescriptions.

### Why does search say "If anyone is in danger, call 911"?
For a few situations where someone could be in danger, like a car accident, smelling gas, a wildfire or being stuck on the road, that line comes first. Everything else follows below it.

### Why isn't a business showing for something urgent?
For an urgent need, a business only shows if its listing has what you'd need to get help: for towing, a phone number. A listing missing it still shows in the directory, but not as an answer when it can't complete the task. If it's your business, claim your listing and add the missing details.

### What shows while I'm typing a search?
Only predictions, so nothing half-finished gets in the way. First **Search for "…"**, then ways your words could carry on (what you typed, with the rest in bold), and sometimes a **Maybe try**: a phrase the search noticed works well, offered as its own suggestion (only from words it understands, never anything personal), then **Go straight to**: what you might be thinking of ("Power out", "Gas for the car"), things whose names start with what you've typed, kinds of things, and towns and counties anywhere in the US. Places come first, right under the site's own tools (ours first, then the bigger towns elsewhere, marked "see what's there"), since where you're looking shapes everything else. It offers as many as match, up to 15, and keeps up with every letter until you stop, press Enter or pick one. While it's still one word ("for"), places and kinds of things come first, then listings and outdoor places that start with it, then stories, groups and experiences. When your words end in a town you're still typing ("moving to sea"), it offers them with each town that fits ("moving to Seattle, WA"). States show too, from two letters or the state's code ("ok" offers **Oklahoma**): "wash" offers **Washington**, the state, and its searches stay on the state, never Washington, DC. When something's name matches only past your Distance setting ("Ford": Show Low Ford, 38 mi, with 10 mi set), one row says how far the nearest is; tap it to widen to that distance. A word search doesn't know, like a model number ("Ford F150"), doesn't stop it: it suggests from the words it knows ("Ford") and the kind of place those names are (car dealers), and asks if you'd like to suggest the new word. When no name has all your words ("hearing test"), it shows the places the full search would find instead, so there's always something to tap. Many places that share a name at one spot ("Big Lake Dam…", "Big Lake Boating…") show as one line, **Big Lake · 9 places**; tap it to see them all. Until you type a space, search treats your last word as unfinished and finishes it the way you most likely mean: "live mu" offers **live music** and shows music venues, "self stor" shows Self Storage. Words that only describe a thing ("new", "live", "best") narrow what it is instead of changing it, so "new music" means music places. When your words clearly mean a kind of place, that kind and its places come first; a business whose name only shares a word comes after (a nurse named Musick isn't music). When many visitors who typed the same words picked the same kind, its line says "most picked": a label only, it never moves anything up. That's counted by the words and the kind only, never who, and each visit counts once, so one person repeating never changes it. When your words name a situation ("can't find my car"), its answer shows right there with the nearest places and **Call**. Tap any of them, or press **Enter**, and the full results appear with Suggestions and Quick Search. Clicking into the box selects what's there, so typing replaces it. **Esc** clears the words; press it again to close. On a phone, the **Location** line at the top also shows what Search Directory and Distance are set to. Tap it to open them, or tap ✎ to change the place. The likeliest come first: a name you've typed in full before one you've only started ("phoenix" puts Phoenix before Phoenixville, which is only 7 of its 12 letters), whole words before part of a word, and what's in the place you've set before the same name elsewhere ("plum" in Show Low offers Plumbers before Plum City, WI). Suggestions start once you've typed three letters; before that, your recent and quick searches show. When nothing fits the letters yet, it says so and Enter still searches them. On a computer, the place at the top of the search has a **×** beside it: tap it to search everywhere.

A word you're still typing is offered as the full words it's becoming, each saying what it would be for: "mexi" offers **New Mexico** (a state to explore), **Mexico** (a country), **Mexican food** (restaurants and Mexican cuisine) and **Mexican culture** (heritage, history, museums, cultural centers); "nava" offers the **Navajo Nation** (its land and visitor information), **Navajo County** and Navajo food and culture. The words come from public sources (Wikidata's countries and their peoples, our tribal nations, U.S. towns and counties), whether or not we list anything there yet; what we have comes first in the results. Words that name a kind of place come first as that kind: "dog park" (or "dog p") offers **Dog parks**, nearest first; "playg" offers Playgrounds. Tap the one you mean. The page you're on sets the order, never what's offered: on Let's Explore places, the outdoors and culture come first; in the Directory, businesses and food come first. Top to bottom, it answers in order: **where** (the Location line, asking there when your words need a place, like "near me"), **what your words mean** (a spelling offer, the full words a partly typed word is becoming, a situation like "Found someone's belongings" with its first answers, and the kinds of place it may mean), then ways to keep typing ("found a dog"; a word not finished yet, like "jalapen", offers the word it finishes), then the places and listings themselves. Press Enter (or the search icon) for every result; on a phone, **See all results for …** at the foot does the same. On the Directory page, **See them all in the directory list** at the foot of the results puts your words into the list and map below; Enter does the same. The words searched stay on the list as a filter ("“Found” ×"); opening the search again starts empty, for new words. Closing the search with × (or Escape, or a tap outside it) clears that filter and shows the whole directory again. After Enter the results open the same way as while typing: a word that starts a situation ("found": Found someone's belongings, Lost or found pet) leads, then everything that matches.

### What happens when I search "near me"?
"Near me", "nearby", "closest" and "close to me" tell search where to look, so they aren't matched as words: "concerts near me" searches for concerts. If you've set a place, it shows the **nearest**, each with how far it is, with no distance cut-off: our listings first, then what OpenStreetMap has. For everyday errands (coffee, groceries, gas) near means within 5 miles; for the outdoors (hikes, camping, fishing, parks), within 30. If fewer than 3 are that near, it says so above the results ("Our results for coffee near Phoenix are lacking: the nearest of ours is 12 mi away"), and we hear about it: the words and the town (never who) go to our team, and the town goes to our crawler to find more. A hike counts by what a place is, not just its name: peaks, canyons, trails, preserves and parks come up for "hikes near me", and so do OpenStreetMap's trailheads. The Location line says **Near you** when the place came from your device. If you haven't set a place, **Where are you?** opens as soon as you type "near me" (and again if you press Enter), and it tries **Auto-detect** straight away (your browser asks permission, once; if you've blocked it, it says so). Or type your town or ZIP. It shows only what sets where you are: no trips, regions or distance, since "near me" is 2 miles around you. Nothing from everywhere shows until you set one, on any page, the business directory included: "near me" only makes sense around you. Just "near me", with nothing else, shows everything near you, nearest first (up to 25; with fewer than 3 of ours, what OpenStreetMap has too), then asks **Is there something specific you'd like to search for?** with one-tap choices (coffee, places to eat, groceries, gas, a pharmacy, things to do, where to stay, parks, health care). Close the picker and the results keep **Set my location** and **Use my location**. While you're still typing "near m", it offers **near me** to finish it. We don't keep your location; it's only used to set the place you search from.

### Why does search ask me to set my location?
The **Location** line at the top of search always shows where it's looking (a town, a distance, or Everywhere), and its pencil changes it: type a town or ZIP, or use your location. When your words need a place to mean anything ("near me", "nearby", "closest") and none is set, search also asks for a place right above the results: tap **Use my location** (the target icon) and it finds you in one tap, or choose a town or ZIP. A situation that needs a place, like "Locked out or lost keys", asks the same way. For other searches with no place set (pizza, a plumber), results come from everywhere and the Location line is where you narrow them.

Wherever a page or the search window asks where, it's one box and one button: type a town or ZIP and pick it (or press Enter on a ZIP), or tap the target icon to use your location. The box shows the place you're searching; × beside it clears it. **More choices** at the foot of the list opens the full picker: regions, your saved places, Distance, State · City · ZIP, Search everywhere and the Trip Planner. Distance comes after the place: it shows only once a place is set, and only for a town, a ZIP or your location (a region already has its own edges). Signed in, the picker offers your saved places and **My default place** instead of a way to sign in.

With no place set, the map never uses your location on its own, even if your browser already allows it. Under the panel's title is the same **Town or ZIP** box as everywhere else: type a town or ZIP, or tap the target to use where you are. A line under it says how the place was chosen: your default place, found by your device (the target), the region you came in through, one of your saved places, or chosen by you. On a page about one area, it says that area is the one shown. Typing in the box changes it. The count of places on the map stays in view; places OpenStreetMap adds are counted after ours. This is the same from the Directory and from Let's Explore. Tick "Always use my location when no place is set" to have the map use your location without asking on this device; the line then says so, with **Ask each time** to undo it.

When your words name a thing, search says what you might want with it, even if no business is named for it: "sea lions" offers **Where to see sea lions** (places that mention them), **Wildlife sanctuaries, zoos and aquariums**, **Help a hurt or stranded animal** and **Learn about sea lions**; a pet offers adopting, care and supplies; a food, where to eat it and where to buy it; a plant, where to see it and garden centers. What it is comes from WordNet, a public dictionary, so it works for any animal, plant or food, not just ones we've listed. Words that name one thing together are matched together: "sea lions" never finds a church "by-the-Sea" or a business in "All Seasons". Two real words one letter off a thing ("seal lions") get **Did you mean sea lions?**, offered, never changed. The kinds of place your words may mean show as buttons; tap one for those places.

When your words describe a situation and name a state's code ("ca tow truck"), search doesn't assume the state; it asks "In California? Where are you?" with a town or ZIP box and the target icon, then shows the nearest towing, roadside repair and gas.

### What happens if I misspell something?
Search never changes your words on its own. If a word isn't one it knows, it asks **Did you mean…?** and offers up to three of the closest words, the likeliest first: the ones that keep the most of what you typed, then the same first letter and the same sound. Each option says what it would find, like "bathroom: A restroom". Tap the one you meant. It asks while you type, too, once a word is finished. It only offers real slips: one letter off in a short word, two in a longer one. A word search understands, like "cardio" or "x-rays", is never "corrected", and neither is a word used here: a town's or a landmark's name, or a word in the names and descriptions of the businesses we list and the pages we read ("Blue Hills" is never "blue halls"). Two letters swapped count as one slip ("motle": motel). The words it offers come first from what's used here, the more often the likelier (our listings, the places named on pages we read, our towns and landmarks), then from the site's own dictionary (the same one behind spell check in forms), so a common word nobody has listed yet still gets offered: "swiming" offers **swimming**. When a word looks like a slip, "Did you mean" comes first, before anything else in the results. A slip always gets an offer: when nothing closer is found, the closest words our listings use are offered ("resturants": restaurants), and words run together are offered apart when they name a kind of place ("icecream": ice cream). While you're still typing a word, finishing it comes first: "jala" offers jalapeno, not a guess like "java"; a guess is offered only when nothing starts with what you've typed. The word it would finish is listed first too, above **Search for** what you've typed so far.

### Why does "mental health" show counselors and psychologists too?
Because they give that care, even when their name doesn't say "mental health". Search knows which kinds of business a phrase covers: "mental health" and "behavioral health" find mental health services and clinics, counselors, counseling services, psychologists, marriage and family counselors, social workers, and addiction and substance abuse treatment; "addiction" and "substance abuse" find the treatment centers. Places named for what you typed come first. Under **Closest kinds** you can tap one kind to see only those.

### How does search know what a word means?
It knows the kinds of place that serve everyday words, even when no business uses the word in its name. "X-rays", "xray" and "x ray" all mean Diagnostic Imaging Centers. "Cardio" and "gym" mean gyms and fitness centers. "Haircut" means barbers and salons. Each kind of business has the everyday words people use for it. An English dictionary (WordNet) and word meanings learned from a large body of everyday writing carry those further: "bread" reaches the bakery and the grocery store, and so do sourdough and bagels; "burrito" finds restaurants; "sedan" finds car dealers. That's worked out ahead of time on our server: your search isn't sent anywhere to be understood. While you type, those kinds show with how many are in your place. After **Enter**, **Closest kinds for "…"** lists them, one tap each. When it's only a loose guess, it says it's not sure and offers the guesses as **Maybe**. If nothing matches by name or kind, search shows what **mentions** your words in its description, then suggests changing your search (fewer words, or another word for it) and offers **Suggest it**. What people open after a search keeps improving it. When people keep searching for a kind of place we don't have yet, like cooking classes, search adds that kind on its own with the words people used, sends our crawler to look for such places in every town we cover (checking each one on its own website), and takes the kind away again if nothing comes of it.

### What happens if search doesn't recognize a word?
It says so instead of guessing: "We don't recognize the word 'frobnicate'", or "…the phrase" when each word is known
but together they don't name anything yet. Tap **Suggest it** and we'll look at it and teach search what it means.
Nothing about you is attached to the suggestion beyond what you type. If it's a slip, search may also ask **Did you
mean…?**, offering only words that fit where yours sits ("how to compost" won't offer "compass").

### What happens to words search didn't understand?
Search learns them. It already knows most everyday words from a dictionary: it knows a purse, a passport and keys are
someone's belongings, and that a hamster is a pet. So "I found someone's purse" or "someone stole my backpack" works
without anyone teaching it. For words the dictionary doesn't have (local names, new slang), search works out what they
mean on its own, from the words around them and from how our listings and trusted public sources use them, with no
person needed. What people tap afterwards never decides what a word means. Our team can see everything it learned and
undo any of it. Only counts are used, never who searched.

### What should I do if I found someone's wallet or lost mine?
Search for it the way you'd say it. "Found a wallet" or "I found someone's phone" shows the police (non-emergency)
near you to hand it in to, and a visitor center or ranger station if you found it on a trail or in a park. "Lost my
wallet" or "my ID was stolen" shows where to report it, where to replace a driver's license, your bank to cancel cards,
and where to replace a phone.

### Can I remove a suggestion that doesn't fit?
Yes. Tap the **×** beside a "Did you mean", a word that finishes your search, or a "May also help" result. It goes
away for you, and it counts toward taking that suggestion away for everyone who searches the same words. Only the
count is kept, never who removed it.

### How does search know which "fall" I mean?
Some words mean different things depending on the words around them, and search reads them that way. "I took a fall" or "fell down the stairs" is an injury, so it shows urgent care and emergency rooms. "Fall festival" or "fall hours" is the season, September to November. "Show Low Falls" is a waterfall. "A tree fell" is storm damage, so it shows tree services. "Feeling like I'm falling" is something you feel, so it shows someone to talk to and clinics. "Spring", "summer" and "winter" work the same way: "spring break" is the season, "hot springs" is a place. Showing only what's on during a season is coming; for now the season is read but doesn't narrow the list yet. If anyone is in danger, call 911.

### Can I search for something I need done, like "fix my chainsaw"?
Yes. Search reads what you want done and shows who does it: "fix my chainsaw" finds small-engine repair, "install a
water heater" finds plumbers and contractors, "register my truck" finds the motor vehicle office, "set up my printer"
finds computer help, "rent a trailer" finds equipment rental, and "building a deck" finds lumber, tools and builders.
If there's a how-to guide for it, it shows first; otherwise search shows the people and places that can do it or help.

### Are there step-by-step how-to guides?
Some, and more as people ask. When enough people search for how to do something ("how to compost leaves"), we gather
the steps from official how-to pages only, such as university extension services and government agencies. Our team
then puts them in our own words and checks each step against its source before the guide shows. A step that still reads as its source wrote it can't be published unless that source is public domain or openly licensed, and nothing is drafted from a site our list of sources marks as cite only. Each step links to
its source. Search for "how to …" and a published guide comes first, followed by who can do it for you. If a step is
wrong or missing, tell us from the guide.

### Can I say when I need it, like "asap" or "this weekend"?
Yes. "Now", "asap", "right away" and "emergency" put places that are open now first. "Tonight", "today", "this
morning" and "this weekend" leave out places known to be closed then. "Fall", "spring", "summer" and "winter" are
read as the season. "Upcoming" and "coming up" are read as a time, not a word to search for; showing upcoming events
is coming.

### What does "May also help" mean in search?
Search learns what words mean, on its own: if the words you used clearly mean a kind of place (from the words themselves and trusted public sources that back it up), that kind can show under your results as **May also help**. What people open afterwards never decides it; it only helps choose between two kinds that fit your words equally well. It learns from counts of the words only, never from who searched. When a place people need is missing nearby, our crawler looks for it in public data and on official government and school websites first. What people search for goes to the front of its list, ahead of its general sweep, and searches in each town add up for the area around it.

### Why does search show phone lines, not just businesses?
Some things that go wrong aren't a business's job: reporting a power outage, a road hazard, or finding local help. For these, search shows **Phone lines and services** from our area guides, with a **Call** button. Every number was found on the provider's own official website when we last checked, and we check them again every month. The full guide for your area is one tap away. If a number is wrong or missing, tap **Tell us** on the guide. If anyone is in danger, call 911.

### Can I search a county?
Yes. Type it with the word "county": "restaurants in Navajo County", "pizza Apache County AZ", "King County WA". Search looks across every town in the county. Your place stays as you set it.

### Does search know towns you don't cover yet?
Yes. Any U.S. city or town you name is the place you're searching, whether or not we have listings or a guide there yet: "pizza in Phoenix", "Dana Point", "hotels in Sedona". Arizona's towns come first when a name is used in more than one state (Glendale is Glendale, Arizona); add the state for another one ("Glendale, CA"). When your words are the exact name of a place we have ("Four Corners Monument", "Laguna Art Museum"), you get that place, not a town that shares its first words. If we don't have much there yet, search says so and offers to build its guide, and asking for it is how we know where to grow next.

### What happens if I search just a place's name?
Search asks what you'd like to know about it: **things to do**, **places to eat**, **places to stay**, **moving there** or **plan a trip**. Tap one to search it there. Your place doesn't change unless you tap **Make it my place**. This works with just the town's name too, like "Seattle". If a town's name is used in several states, like Springfield, search asks **Which Springfield?** and lists them.

### Can search take me to the Trip Planner?
Yes. Type what you're planning, like "planning", "plan a trip", "road trip" or "vacation", and the **Trip Planner** shows first while you type, set to your place. Press **Enter** and search asks if you were looking for it, above the results. If your words name something else too, like "planning and zoning", the offices come first and the Trip Planner follows them. Saving and sharing trip plans is coming.

### What happens if I search for "help"?
We ask which help you mean: **help using Traversence** (the Help Center and FAQ) or **help in your place** (assistance, services and support). If you type what kind of help, like "legal help" or "housekeeping help", we search for local services straight away.

### What happens if search can't tell what I mean?
It says so instead of guessing. You won't get a list of things that just happen to share a word ("standing on the corner" won't find a business with "corner" in its description). If your words point to a situation, like "stuck" or "leaking", it offers those under **Maybe you mean**. Otherwise, name the kind of place or service (plumber, pizza, campground), or say what's going on (stuck in the mud, the power went out). You don't have to press Enter: pause for a moment while typing and the results, or where your words are heading, appear.

### Does Traversence keep what I search for?
Not who searched it. We count the words people search, by area and day, and how those searches turned out (found something, found nothing, or searched again), for words typed but not searched, whether a suggestion was tapped or the search was closed, and what kind of thing was opened afterwards (a tire shop, a pizza place, never which one you are), so we can see what search doesn't understand yet and fix it. No account, device or location is kept with them, and words only show to our team once several searches used them. Your recent searches stay in your own browser.

### Why does search ask "Where are you?"
For something you need nearby, like a restroom, gas or a tow, "nearest" only means something once we know where you are. Tap **Choose your place or use Auto-detect** and the nearest places show. Auto-detect doesn't share where you are. Once you've set a place, search uses it. That includes a city, state or ZIP outside our regions, like Seattle, so it won't ask again.

### I set a place outside your regions. What does search show?
It searches there. Naming the place in your search works the same way, even while you type: "moving to Seattle, WA" or "moving to Seattle Washington" answers for Seattle without changing your place. When the words are both a town and the start of a name we have ("four corners": Four Corners, Wyoming, and **Four Corners Monument**), the name is offered too, however far it is from that town. If we have no listings there yet, search says so ("We have no listings in Seattle, WA yet") instead of showing places hundreds of miles away. For everyday businesses (places to eat, coffee, places to stay, gas, groceries, pharmacies, hospitals and clinics, self storage, auto repair, hardware and laundry), it shows a few nearby from **OpenStreetMap**, the free, open map, whenever we have none within your Distance setting, anywhere in Arizona, New Mexico, Utah, Colorado and Nevada ("coffee near me" in Phoenix shows Phoenix coffee shops, not ours 90 miles away). Outside those five states there's none of OpenStreetMap's to show: we read our own weekly copy of OpenStreetMap for them, and since 2026-11-19 we no longer ask OpenStreetMap's servers for anywhere else (see "Why doesn't Traversence ask OpenStreetMap's servers any more?"). They're marked as OpenStreetMap's, not our listings, with what OpenStreetMap knows (what it is, how far, its address, phone and hours when it has them); a tap opens directions. It covers the outdoors too: trailheads, parks and preserves, viewpoints and campgrounds ("hiking near me" shows trailheads), never anything marked historic or that may be a sacred or protected place. The same search also sends the town to our crawler, which finds its places to eat, stay, do, shop and health care on the map and on each one's own website, and checks them like every listing, so real listings usually follow within an hour or two. Landmarks and attractions never come from there. For everything else, and for more, you can carry on your search on **Google Maps** with one tap: one link for each thing you need (for a move: movers and trucks, storage, utilities, places to live), or one for what you typed ("pizza near Seattle, WA"). It opens in a new tab with only your words and the town. Nothing about you goes with it, and we only count that a link was tapped in that town. Tap **Choose another place** to pick another one.

### What are the lines that finish my words as I type?
They're ways your search could carry on, like "where can i park **overnight for free**" or "parks **for kids**". What you typed is plain and the rest is bold. Tap one to search it. They come from the situations we know, the ways people ask ("…check out", "…worth the drive") and what fits the thing you named (open now, this weekend, near your place). They don't come from other people's searches.

### Why doesn't "parks" show RV parks?
Search reads the kind of thing you mean. "Parks" are parks, including state and national parks. RV parks and mobile home parks are their own kinds, so search for those by name ("RV parks"). "Where can I park" is about parking: places to stay overnight with an RV or camper, rest areas and truck stops, parking lots, and who to call about parking rules.

### Can I search near a lake, park or landmark?
Yes. "Plumber near Lyman Lake" searches about 15 miles around the lake, and if there are none that close, the nearest ones beyond follow. Your own place stays as you set it.

### If I search a business by name, can I find others like it?
Yes. A business named in full shows first, with what it offers and **More like it nearby**.

### What if nothing matches all my words?
If no place has every word you typed, **Suggestions** lists each word on its own with how many it finds, like **Search "hat" · 1 found**. Tap one to search for it.

### Does searching change the place I set?
No. Search starts in the place you set and never changes it. Results in your place come first. Anything that matches beyond it follows under **Beyond (your place)**, nearest first, with how far away it is. If you chose a town, like "Saint Johns, AZ", that town comes first, and the rest of its area follows under **Beyond**. To search somewhere else for one search, say so: "pizza in Show Low". Your place stays as you set it. A town outside our regions counts only when you say "in" or "near" it, or name its state.

### Can I save my own search phrases?
Yes. In the search panel, **⊕** next to **Quick Search** adds the words you've typed as one of your quick searches, and **✎** lets you add, change or remove them. Each shows how many it finds where you are, counted the way its search would find them (none for what changes by the minute, like open now). For now they're saved in this browser. To count them, the words are sent with the search; each count is kept for an hour by phrase and place, never with who asked, and then cleared. To keep a search on your account, with its place, use **☆ Save** on its results.

### How do I switch between the list and the map?
Use **List**, **Table** or **Map** at the top right of Get Local (a phone has List and Map). **Map** opens full screen with every result as a pin; **Exit map** brings you back to the list, which keeps any filters you changed on the map. **Table** shows the same results as rows: tap a column's name to sort it (A to Z, Z to A, or nearest first), search it, or untick values to hide them ("Show Restaurants (6)"); the column menus work on the results on the page, and **Clear the column filters** brings every row back. On a computer you may see the list **beside the map** instead: the numbered businesses on the left, their numbered pins on the right, a dashed ring for how far you chose, and **Search this area** once you move the map. We're trying both layouts to see which helps people more (we count which one a device has and what's tapped there, never who); **Arrange** lets you pick the one you like.

### Where do I find the guide to a town or area?
On Let's Explore, with the place set, **The guide to …** sits at the top of Journeys & guides: its towns, places to go, the outdoors and local businesses. Searching the town's name opens it too. A town with no guide yet offers **Build the guide** in search: a first look in about two minutes, the full guide after.

Each business in a guide shows what it is, its street, and whether it's open now when we know its hours. Our crawler reads these from the business's own website and checks them again every month or so; one we haven't read yet shows its name, kind and distance until it's been read.

### What can I do on the map?
- **Search:** type in the search at the top and press Enter. The pins change to match.
- **The list, nearest first:** the panel lists everything on the map, ours and OpenStreetMap's together, nearest first with the miles: from where you are (the blue dot, or the location you've shared with the site), else from the place you've set, else from the middle of the map. The line above the list says which. A business we only know by its ZIP code shows "about … mi · location approx." and comes after the ones we know exactly at about the same distance. Tap one of ours to choose its pin and see its card; one from OpenStreetMap opens directions. **Show 25 more** goes further.
- **Controls** (bottom right): zoom in and out, **Go to my location** (the arrow: it moves the map to where you are), **Search this area**, and **Map style**.
- **Where it opens:** in a 5-mile circle around your place when your place has a point (Auto-detect, or a ZIP), or, with no place set, around you (your browser asks once whether to share your location); widen with the distance or **Search this area**. Opened for one place, it opens there.
- **A pin's card:** its photo when it has one (credited), what you'd do there (**Walk**, **Drive**, **Hike**, **Exhibits**, **Views**, **On the water**, **Camp**, **Picnic & play**, **Historic site**, from what the place is), a few lines on what it is (from Wikipedia; for a place without an article, the first time anyone opens it Claude, the AI we use, writes two or three sentences from what we hold and public sources, kept for everyone after; until then, what Wikidata records: whether it's on the National Register, when it dates from, its style and architect), then, when one of our guides tells its story, the guide's own words about it (**From the Glendale guide ›**), where it is, hours, and **Call**, **Directions**, **Website** and **Open**. Been there? **Add a tip or review** or **Suggest a change** sits right with it. Places are ranked by the interest they draw (how much people read about them on Wikipedia, how many Wikipedias cover them, and the guides and journeys that name them), then by distance.
- **Where you are:** a blue dot marks where you are as soon as the map opens, if your browser already shares your location with Traversence (or your place came from Auto-detect). It doesn't ask on its own; tap **My location** for that. Your location stays on your device.
- **Moved the map?** A **Search this area** button appears at the top, in words, so you can look where the map now is.
- **Nothing of ours there yet?** The panel offers **Search this area**, **Widen to 25 mi**, and what OpenStreetMap has there as lighter pins, not our listings (in Arizona, New Mexico, Utah, Colorado and Nevada, from our own copy of OpenStreetMap; elsewhere there are none): with no words, everything everyday at once (places to eat, coffee, groceries, gas, pharmacies, places to stay); or one kind at a tap, trails, parks and campgrounds included. Tap a pin for what it is, its address, phone, hours and directions. If your words name a kind ("coffee shops"), those show at once. Asking also sends that town to our crawler, so our own listings follow, usually within an hour or two.
- **The panel:** the tab on the map's left edge (the bottom on phones) opens it. It has what's shown, the distance, "Search this area as I move the map", the map style, and the details of the place you tapped. Use the arrows there to step to the next place nearby.
