- **Folding the panel (phones):** the chevron at the panel's top right folds it down to its title, so you can see more map. Tap the title or the chevron to bring it back. Tapping a pin brings it back too.

### What does Auto-detect do?
It sets your place to the town you're in, by its name ("St. Johns"), not the wider area around it, right away: searches look at that town and the miles around it, and the directory follows it. Let's Explore shows the town's name, with the area it's in under it, since its pages cover the whole area. Set your place with it before this change? Tap the target once more. If it can't name the area, it says so; pick a region or type a ZIP instead. To change it, tap the place again.

### Does "My location" share where I am?
No. It only moves the map on your device. It isn't saved or sent anywhere.

### What do the hearts on the map mean?
A heart in a circle is one of your favorites: a business you linked or saved to your Address Book. Favorites always show on their own, never grouped with other pins.

### What map styles are there?
- **Streets** and **Light** (a faded map, so pins stand out) cover everywhere.
- **Terrain** (topographic) and **Satellite** (with towns and roads labelled) cover the United States.

The map remembers the style you chose.

### I set Distance to "My place only" and nothing came up. Now what?
Search says so and tells you how far the nearest one is ("Nothing in Saint Johns. The nearest is 23 mi away."), with buttons for the next distances out, like **25 mi**, **50 mi** and **No limit**, each showing how many it finds. The same happens at any distance, including the 10 mi it starts at ("Nothing within 10 mi of St. Johns. The nearest is 40 mi away."). Tap one and the Distance slider moves with it. Search never looks further on its own. Your place stays as you set it.

### Why do some category names look shorter than in other directories?
Business categories often carry trade codes, like "Automobile Parts & Supplies-Retail-New" or "Feed-Dealers (Whls)". We show the plain name ("Automobile Parts & Supplies", "Feed-Dealers"). "Used" stays, because it tells you something.

### What if there's nothing like it where I am?
The search tells you: "No 'pizza' in St. Johns yet." You can **Recommend one**, or **Add a listing** if it's yours. Below that, it shows the nearest towns that do have it, nearest first. Tap one to see it on the map with what's around it.

### How do I filter the directory?
In Get Local, the line over the list is the filter: tap its kind, distance or place to change them, × to clear one. **Filters** beside it has **Open now**, **Has a phone**, **Has a website** and **★ Favorites**: tick one and it shows in the line, with × to turn it off. Elsewhere, tap the search at the top: at the foot of **Quick Search** are the same filters, with **Near me**.

### How do I arrange Get Local's results?
Tap **Arrange** beside Filters. **Results per page**: 10, 25, 50 or 100. **Show**: businesses closed for good show last, marked Closed; untick it to hide them for you (we never hide them for everyone). **On each card**: which details each business shows and in what order (its kind, distance, hours, address, phone number, website or email); drag them, or use ↑ ↓, × to take one off, **+ Add a detail** to bring one back. The first three share the top line. **On a computer**: the list beside the map, or List, Table or Map. It's remembered on this device, and **Put it all back as it was** undoes it all.

### What are the buttons on a business's card?
**Call** and **Directions** are the round buttons by its name; **⋯** holds **Email**, **Website** and **Peek** (a quick card without leaving the page), each in words, and says so when a business hasn't given an email or a website yet. The link button on the right links the business to your account. The small number beside the name is how sure we are of the listing's details: tap ⓘ to see why.

### Why doesn't a business show Directions?
Its only address is a mailbox (a PO Box or a private mailbox), so there's nowhere to drive to: the card says **Mailing address only**, and its pin on the map marks its town, not a street. If it's your business, add a street address in your listing's editor and Directions appears. Places that keep their address private for the safety of the people they help, like shelters, never show one: call them instead.

### What order are Get Local's results in?
**Best match**, unless you choose otherwise under **Sort**: if you typed words, the closest matches first; then the businesses we're most certain of (how sure we are that a listing's details are right: a business that has claimed its listing and confirmed it rises to the top); then the nearest. **Near me** is always the nearest first, with how many miles. **Nearest first** and **A to Z** are under Sort too.

### How do I change where I'm looking?
Tap the place at the top left of the directory (📍 and the place name) and choose another. To clear it and search everywhere, tap the **×** beside it.

### Why is the search at the top only a ⌕ on Get Local?
Get Local has its own search bar, so the one at the top folds to its ⌕ to keep one search on the page. Tap it (or press Ctrl K, ⌘K on a Mac) to search the whole site: places, stories and groups as well as businesses. On other pages the top search shows in full, with the place you're searching in beside it (tap it to change where).

### In what order are businesses listed?
For a town or "Near me", nearest first. Otherwise, the best match for what you searched.

### Can I comment on a business, a place or an experience?
Yes. Most pages have **Comments** at the bottom: share a tip, or say what it was like. Signed in, the box is right there, with a few ideas to start from on a business (best thing to order, parking, good for kids, accessible?): tap one and it starts your comment for you. Not signed in, tapping the box offers to sign in or make an account. You need to be 18 or older. Comments are public and show your name. You can remove your own, and report one that breaks the rules. Stars and ratings aren't used: say what you think in words.

### What is my gallery?
Every photo and video you keep on Traversence, in one place: open **Gallery** in your Dashboard menu. It's grouped by year and month, by when each photo was taken (six a month, then **View all**), and photos from your journeys and your profile appear there too. **Upload** adds photos and videos (a video can be up to 1 GB; your phone makes photos a sensible size first, and the place a photo was taken is never sent). Tap a photo to see it full screen: swipe or use the arrows, pinch to zoom, and tap the dark background, press Esc or tap × to close. **Select** lets you put several in an album, or delete ones you uploaded (photos from journeys or your profile are removed where they live). Open one and its ⋯ menu edits your photo's caption, its alt text (a description for people who use screen readers) and when it was taken, or deletes it. A business has a gallery too (see below), and an album can be a Portfolio for clients or Brand & documents.

### Who can see my albums?
You choose for each album, under **Edit & who sees it**: **Public** (anyone, shown on your profile's Gallery tab), **Unlisted** (only people with its link or QR code; search engines don't list it) or **Private** (only you and the people you invite). **Share** gives you the link, your phone's share sheet and a QR code. What you upload is private until you put it in an album you share. Open any photo and it says who can see it, from the most open album it's in: **Public**, **Anyone with the link**, **Invited people**, or **Only you** (**Your team** for a business's photos); a journey or profile photo follows its own page. **New link** gives an album a fresh link at once, and making it private does the same, so any link or QR code you shared before stops working, photos included. Deleting an album keeps its photos in your gallery.

### How do I add photos or a video to my business or my profile?
Open your listing (as its owner or manager) or your profile, and tap **Photos & video** on the picture at the top. Add up to 8 photos, or link a YouTube or Vimeo video, and choose which shows first. Use your own photos, or ones you have permission to use. We keep a resized copy and nothing else from the file, such as where it was taken. On a private profile, only your connections see them.

### What do the views and likes on a page mean?
Views count visits that saw the page. Likes count people who liked it. We never show who viewed or liked anything. Likes are checked for automated activity, so a count means real people: likes that come at a machine's pace, or more likes than a page has views, are taken off the count, and anything unusual is looked at by our team. **Share** sends the page with your phone's share options, or copies its link.

### Does Traversence track when I call or visit a business's website?
We count it for the business, never who did it. A tap on Call, Directions, Website or a contact page adds one to that business's count for the day. No name, account, device or address is stored with it. Businesses see the totals for the last 30 days.

### What does Traversence count about how the site is used?
Totals only, never who. We count how many visits see each section, listing, journey and place. We also count how people move between sections, and what they search for in which place, with how many results it found. No name, account, device or address is stored with a count. Search words are lower-cased, with email addresses and long numbers removed, and counted by area and day so search can learn what people here look for and what's missing. They're only shown in our reports once 3 or more visits searched them. Rarer ones are deleted after 90 days. Our own team's visits aren't counted, and a business's own people looking at their listing aren't counted on it, so the numbers are visitors'. Businesses see visits to their listing in Business Insights.

### Why is a pin marked "approximate"?
Until we've placed a listing's street address, its pin sits in the middle of its ZIP area. When you open it on the map, we look the address up and move the pin if it matches. Rural addresses don't always match an exact spot. Mileposts, roads without numbers and mail-only addresses are examples. When we can only place a listing near where it is, we say so.

### A pin is in the wrong place. How do I fix it?
Tap **Wrong spot? Fix the pin** on the map card or the listing page (it shows where there's a pin to fix, so not on a PO box). Drag the pin to the door (or tap the map, or tap **I'm here now** if you're there), then **Send**. You don't need an account. We check every suggestion before the map changes. If you run the business, or you're Traversence staff, your pin is saved at once, and you can change the address too.

### I own the business. How do I change our address or pin?
Open **Location and map pin** from Business in your Dashboard (or from the listing page). Change the address and the pin follows it, or drag the pin to your door and save. Only the business's owners and managers, and Traversence staff, can do this.

### Why doesn't a listing have a map pin?
A few reasons:
- The address is a PO box or mail-only, so there's no place to pin.
- We haven't confirmed the spot yet.
- The business asked to keep its address confidential. Those listings show a phone number only and never appear on the map.

### Where does a business's information come from?
From the evidence. Each listing's details (name, address, phone, website, hours) come from what we can find about the business: its own website, official registries, and other pages that match it by name and address or phone. When those disagree with what we had, **the evidence wins**: the value with the most independent sources goes on the listing by itself. Our team can see every change and undo it. Google, Yelp and Facebook pages only confirm what we found; we never copy from them. Every source we use is on our list with its licence, and the licence decides what we may do with it: keep its facts, show it with credit, link to it, or only use it to confirm. OpenStreetMap, for example, only tells us a place exists and links to it; Wikipedia gives us facts, never its words.

Once a business is **claimed and confirmed** (✓ Verified), its owner decides. Nothing we find changes it on its own. While a claim is only **Managed by owner** (step 1 of claiming), what we find can still update the name, phone, address and website, but never anything the owner wrote. Instead the owner sees **We found other information about your business** on their listing, with each value and where we found it, and can **Use this** or **Dismiss** it.

### Why does my listing say "We found other information about your business"?
Only you see it, as the listing's owner. It lists details we found about your business elsewhere (your website, an official registry, another page that matches you) that differ from your listing. Your listing keeps what you set. Tap **Use this** to put a value on your listing, or **Dismiss** to set it aside; dismissed values aren't offered again.

### What does Traversence show on a tribal nation's land?
What is public: established boundaries, key resources and services, public businesses and publicly known places, each with the nation's own name and its rules for visitors. What is sensitive or specific to the nation, such as sacred, burial or ceremonial places and its springs, is not shown. It stays that way until the nation has its own controls on Traversence and decides. When a nation asks us to close something, it stays closed until the nation asks to reopen it.

### Where does the information about a landmark or outdoor place come from?
- **Landmarks:** the name, the kind of feature and the map point come from the federal names registry (USGS Geographic
  Names). The description comes from Wikidata and Wikipedia, credited on the page.
- **Outdoor places** (campgrounds, trailheads, recreation areas): Recreation.gov, the federal recreation registry. That
  covers who runs the place, what you can do there, its phone and its page. It's refreshed each month.
- **Phone lines in a resource guide:** our staff enter them. A line shows only when its number was found on the
  provider's own official page at the last check.
- **On a tribal nation's land:** we keep only the places the nation has opened to visitors. Nothing is kept about the
  rest.

Events will work the same way when they're added. Marketplace posts come from the people who post them (see Buying and selling).

### Why doesn't a landmark have a photo?
A landmark's photo comes from Wikimedia Commons, a free library of photos, credited to the person who took it with its licence. If the landmark's own entry has none, we look on Commons for one taken there: free to use, named for the landmark, and taken nearby (or naming its state, county or town). We look every hour, a few landmarks at a time, and look again a month later for ones with nothing yet. You can also add yours with **Share a photo** on the landmark's page.

### Where do a listing's phone number and website come from?
From the business's own website first, then official registries (like the NPI Registry for health care and the IRS
list of nonprofits), then other public pages that show the business with its name and address. A number goes on a
listing when the business's own website, a registry or a government page gives it, or when two separate websites
agree; otherwise our staff check it first. Google, Yelp and Facebook pages are links that tell us where to look,
never a source on their own: we link to them rather than copy what they say. If you own the business, claim your listing to set your own
details: once your claim is confirmed, they're never overwritten.

### I own a business. How do I get it listed?
Use **Add or claim a business** at the bottom of any page. If your business is already listed, claim it. If it isn't, add it yourself: it takes a few minutes from your phone, and it's live as soon as you confirm a code we email you.

### Can I add my business if it only has a Facebook page, or no website at all?
Yes. A website isn't needed. Give its name, what it is, the town, a phone number, and a street address, or tap **Use where I am now** to drop a pin if there's no usable address. Add your Facebook page, website, hours and what you offer if you like, and photos once it's live. Your Traversence listing can be your business's page on the web.

Before saving, we check whether it's already listed (the same phone, website or Facebook page, or a similar name in the same town). If it is, we show you and you can claim that listing instead, so there aren't two.

### How do you decide which category a business is in?
From what the business says about itself: what its own website calls it, its name, what it offers and its description, matched as whole words (so a "spa" is never found inside "newspaper"), and from which industry those words point to. When it's clearly one category, that's the one; when it isn't, a person picks. A word that happens to be in a name ("Dollar Tree" isn't a tree service) never decides on its own. We also check the businesses already listed and correct a category that plainly doesn't fit (a school district filed under nurses); anything less certain goes to our team, and a category an owner or our team chose is never changed. If yours is wrong, claim your listing and set it, or use **Suggest a change** on the listing.

### Can my business be in more than one category?
Yes. Every business has one **main category**, shown on its card, and can be listed under **up to 4 more**. On the add-your-business form or in your business dashboard, tap **+** beside the category to add another, then type what it is ("movie theater", "event venue", "party rentals") and pick it from the list. Can't find yours? Pick the closest and tell us; we add new categories as businesses need them. People searching any of those categories find you there too, after the businesses whose main category it is. No plan gets more categories or ranks higher: they're the same for everyone. Pick the ones that match what you really do; that's where they work best.

### What should I put in "What you offer"?
Short names of what people come to you for, one per line: "Matinees", "Private screenings", "Concessions". Anyone searching for one of them finds you, even if it isn't a category. Your business dashboard suggests a few that similar businesses list.

### What name should I use for my business?
The name on your sign works best. What you do ("Movies, Popcorn & Parties") is better in your categories and in "What you offer", where people's searches find it. We'll suggest this if your name looks like it carries a list of services, but it's your choice.

### What do I need to add or claim a business?
A Traversence account (18 or older) with:
- **a confirmed email**: the link we email you when you join (you can ask for it again from the page);
- **a phone number**: it stays private, only our team sees it, so we can reach you about your listing;

To add a business, the form also asks for **the business's phone and email**. Your account email is filled in; change it if the business has its own. The business email isn't shown on your listing.

If you're not signed in, the page lets you sign in or create an account and keeps what you've typed.

### Where do the listings come from?
From public information: the business's own website, official records such as the federal list of health-care providers (the NPI Registry), open map data, and directories like chambers of commerce. Our crawler gathers what's there, checks that a business is real (its own website, an official record, a government page, two separate sources, or one source whose page says enough about it: that one shows as unconfirmed until more agree), puts it in the category that fits what it says it is, and joins it with what's already listed instead of making a second copy. Each listing goes up unclaimed, ready for its owner to claim. Each fact keeps where it came from, from the first time our crawler sees a business: what it found and which other sources agreed are kept together, and stay with the listing once it goes up (or with the listing it turned out to be). A business we haven't listed yet isn't forgotten either, so the next time it comes up we already know what we've seen. Open map data (OpenStreetMap) only tells our crawler where to look; what we keep is what the business's own site or an official record says.

### How often does your crawler read a website?
Only as often as it changes. Our crawler keeps a note of each page it reads (its address, when, and a fingerprint of its text, never a copy of the page) and asks the website first whether the page has changed; a page that hasn't isn't downloaded again. Pages that change often, like an events calendar, are read again within a week or two, pages that don't up to every three months, and never more often than the site's robots.txt allows. It reads a page's own structured data first (schema.org, the same markup search engines read), so a business that puts its name, address, phone and hours there is read just as it wrote them. On a page that lists many places, like a county's services page or a chamber's members, each one is read as its own place, with the kind of business its words describe and its town. Search finds the businesses named on those pages even before they're listed (see below).

### Can your crawler take a website off a listing by mistake?
It shouldn't, and we've made sure a missed visit never counts. Our crawler works in short runs, and a run sometimes ends before it reaches everything it set out to check. Until now, a website it never got to could be counted as one that didn't load, and after two of those it came off the listing. Now a check that wasn't done is simply done later: a website comes off only when our crawler actually tried it twice, at least three days apart, and it didn't load. The same goes for the pages we read: a visit that ends early keeps everything we already knew from that website, and each page replaces only what that same page said before. Guides are protected the same way. If a guide's draft doesn't finish, the guide stays as it was, and one our team took down stays down. A section someone on our team saves while Claude is writing is kept as they wrote it. The monthly load of natural landmarks never deletes a place that's held for a person or a tribal nation to decide on. We're now checking the websites taken off before this change and putting back those that load (coming). If you see a listing missing its website, tap **Suggest an edit** on the listing and tell us.

### How does your crawler choose which websites to read?
On its own, for most of them. It meets websites through the pages it reads: when a county page or a chamber's list links to another site, that site joins its list too (a link from a business's name on the page is taken as that business's own website). On each site, it reads the site's map of its pages (its sitemap) and its new pages first, so over a few visits it covers the whole site, not just its front pages. Once a month it also reads the free public web archive Common Crawl, which captures billions of pages: from it, it finds every town, county and state office site in the states we cover, and reads up to 50 pages of each website we read regularly without visiting the site itself. Since 2026-11-20 it finds those pages in Common Crawl's URL Index (its index files for bulk reading, as Common Crawl asks), one request at a time, never by querying its index server in bulk; that server is asked only for a single page's copy when a site doesn't load, at most 12 a run. Every website it meets goes on its list with what kind of site it is, from its address and from what the site says about itself (its title and description). A site that said nothing the first time is asked again two weeks later, and any site still waiting for a decision is looked at again every two months. A **Visit website** link next to a business on a page we read fills in that business's website, rather than being treated as a site to read. When our team reads a site through Listing Intake, it counts as a visit too: its pages go in the crawler's notes, the site joins its list, and the places it names are kept for search where the site's terms allow (never from directories we only cite, or a site our team set aside). Public bodies, like government offices, counties, cities and schools, are read regularly from the start, since what they publish is public. Commercial directories and social media are never copied, only cited. Chambers of commerce, tourism sites, organizations and other sites are classified by the crawler itself from their address and what their home page says (coming: it runs once our indexer is switched on), and read regularly; what they say is kept only as far as their terms and licence allow. Nothing waits on a person any more, except tribal nations' websites, which are always a person's decision. Our team can change any call, and theirs always stands. It reads PDFs as well as web pages, like a county's list of transfer stations or a poster of hours. A PDF that's a picture of a page (a scanned flyer) is read by a text scanner (AWS Textract), and a page that only shows its text in a full browser is read through a service that renders it (Tavily), each within a small monthly budget. Neither is ever used on a page a website asks crawlers not to read.

### Does your crawler keep every link it finds?
Yes, as a note, not as a copy (coming: our indexer is built and switched off until we start it). Every link on every page our crawler reads goes into its index, on the same website or another, with the page it came from and the words of the link, so nothing it has seen is lost; there's no longer a limit of 60 links a page or 40 other websites a visit. Keeping a link isn't reading it. A page is read only when its website may be read, its robots.txt allows it, and at a polite pace (one page every second and a half at least, or slower if the site asks). Facebook and Instagram pages are never read, sign-in and account pages never, and a tribal nation's website only when a person on our team says so. A website that blocks us is never tried again by itself; our team decides. A link to a website about somewhere outside the states we cover (another state's own site, like a Kansas city's, or another country's) is kept in the index as **Outside our region** and never read; if our team welcomes that website, it's read like any other (2026-11-19). Pages near the places we already list are read first, then official and first-party websites, then the rest, so the index grows from what matters to people here. What we learn from a page comes back to the index: a page that gave us real places ranks its links higher, and junk (a parked domain, an endless calendar, a search-results page) is remembered as junk by a short fingerprint, so it's never read twice. The indexer works all the time in small batches, so our website never feels it.

### How can your team see where a listing's details came from?
Every fact we hold about a business (its name, address, phone, hours, website) keeps each source that gave it: the business itself, our team, an official record, or a page our crawler read, with the date. For a page, it also shows how it was read (its own structured data, an item on a list, a scanned flyer) and what that website's terms let us do with it. When a page we read names a business we already list, what the page says joins the listing's record as one more source, so where sources disagree, our team sees it at once.

### Do some websites count for more than others?
Yes, by their record. Each website our crawler uses keeps a count of how often what it showed was later borne out (the business was confirmed and listed) and how often it was shown wrong (the business had closed, or never existed). A site that's right nine times in ten counts for more toward the two independent sources a listing needs; one that's often wrong counts for less, or not at all. A duplicate or a business outside what we list doesn't count against a site. Our team can see each site's record and change how it's used.

### What happens when your crawler can't confirm a business?
The free steps come first: our own index, open map data, official records and the business's own website. When they can't settle it, a paid step can, within a monthly budget we set: Claude (an AI from Anthropic) looks the business up on the web and says whether it's open, closed or not found, giving the pages it relied on and the words on them. We check those words are really on those pages before anything counts. A business confirmed this way is listed under the same rules as any other; one shown to have closed is set aside, with the page that says so, and our team can undo it. Places on tribal nations' land, and places whose location is kept private, are always left to a person. Claude also reads the terms of websites like chambers of commerce, to decide whether our crawler may read them regularly or only link to them, quoting the sentence it relied on.

### How does search show it understood me?
At the top of the results, in one line: "Read as a kind of business: Gun Clubs, near St. Johns", "Read as a name", "Read as a need" or "Read as something to do". If that isn't what you meant, the line offers other readings one tap away: another kind your words could mean, or just the words as you typed them. Ask about one thing, like "Blue Hills Motel hours" or "phone for Joe's Diner", and the answer comes first: the hours or the phone, from the listing, or from the page we read it on, with a link to that page.

### What is "From pages we read" in my search results?
Pages our crawler reads regularly whose titles match your words, from sites in the place you're searching: a county notice, a calendar of events, a list of services. Each opens the page itself, so you can read it where it was published. We keep a note of each page (its title, its address, when we read it), never a copy of it.

### What is "Named on pages we read" in my search results?
Businesses our crawler found on pages it reads regularly, like a county's services page or a chamber's members, that aren't Traversence listings yet. They show when we have few listings of our own for what you searched, nearest first, each with the page it's named on (tap it to see that page) and Call or Directions where the page gave a phone or address. Each one is on its way through the same checks as any business we find: once another independent source agrees (its own website, an official record, a map), it's listed; until then it stays here, never mixed in with our listings. Know one of them is closed or wrong? Use **Tell us** under the results.

The same rules as our listings apply to them. A place that may be sacred, a burial place or an archaeological site isn't shown here unless it's plainly open to visitors (a museum, a park, a historic site), and on a tribal nation's land only businesses show, with the nation's name and a reminder that visitors follow its rules. A shelter's address is never shown, only its phone. A name that reads as a person's (a contact on a county page, say) isn't shown either. Nothing is thrown away: what isn't shown is kept, marked, for our team and, once their tools exist, the nations.

### Do you send anything private to Google's Gemini?
No. We may use Google's Gemini (its free tier) only to read public web pages into facts, such as a business's hours on its own website, and that's switched off until our team turns it on. Before a page is sent, people's private details (emails, ID numbers, anyone named in a death notice, a court record or with a home address) are taken out, and so is any name we keep back: a sacred, burial or archaeological place, a place specific to a tribal nation, a confidential shelter. What it's asked is written by our system from our own lists of places and kinds of business, never your words. Your searches, posts, messages and anything you type to AI on Traversence go only to Claude, and only as our privacy policy and your AI & Learning setting allow. Gemini never finds businesses for us, and nothing it says becomes a fact on a listing until a public record, an open dataset or the business's own website confirms it.

### Why doesn't a domestic-violence shelter show its address?
To keep the people inside safe. Domestic-violence shelters and safe houses don't take walk-ins: you call, they talk with you, and they arrange where to meet. So their listing shows their phone, and **Need help now?** with 911, the National Domestic Violence Hotline (1-800-799-7233, 24 hours) and its text line (text START to 88788). If a shelter publishes an office address itself, that shows. Calls and texts go straight from your phone; we don't see or keep them. Their listings are also never sent to a search or map service to be checked: we check them against our own records only. Every other place, including homeless and family shelters, crisis centers and care homes, shows its address.

### How do you know a listing is right?
Our crawler checks each business against public sources: its own website, official records, government pages and directories. Open map data (OpenStreetMap) only tells it where to look: its phone, hours and address are never copied, only what the business's own website or another source we read says. A business found on just one website can be listed when that page gives enough (its street address and phone, say); it's marked unconfirmed and is checked again and again until more sources agree. A town someone searches for is looked at again a month later, and for the kind of place they searched (storage, auto repair, pharmacies) when that isn't one we already look for there. When its own website lists its locations, that wins for each location's phone and hours. When a source shows the business at another address, it isn't listed at the old one until that's sorted out. Each listing shows when it was listed and when it was last checked. Our crawler reads each listing's own website again every month while we're less sure of it (under 51) and every three months once we're surer, and Google is checked at least every three months: it fills in what's missing (never changing what a person wrote), and checks that the business is still there. When the website now shows a different phone number or different hours from the listing, our team gets the new ones to check (a site's number can be a head office's, and hours can be one season's), and the listing changes once they confirm. It also checks the listings against each other for one business listed twice (see "How do you keep a business from being listed twice?"); two businesses sharing one number are left as two. A website that stops working, twice a few days apart, comes off the listing (or goes to our team, if the owner entered it), and it stays off until the business's site works again or someone puts it back. Every change to a listing, by our crawler, our team, the owner or a visitor's accepted fix, is kept with who or what made it, and what it replaced is kept too, so nothing we learned is lost and an old value never quietly comes back. A listing's phone, address or website is changed by what other sources say only when two independent sources agree (one website counts once), and a business's name only ever gets spelled out in full, never swapped for another name. When two listings turn out to be one business, what we knew about both is kept together on the one that stays. When a business's own website says it has closed for good, its listing shows **Possibly closed**, quoting what the site says, with **Still open? Tell us**, and our team checks. It shows Closed (still findable, with **Reopened? Tell us**) only once a person or the owner confirms it: one website alone never closes a listing. If the site stops saying so, the notice goes. When its website says it has moved, our team gets the new address to check (never changed on its own, since one branch moving isn't the business moving). When its website doesn't load for us, we try again a few days later and look for Common Crawl's latest copy of it (a free public archive of the web): if that copy is recent, the site is probably up and refusing our crawler, so our team checks it instead of the website coming off the listing, and a missing description can come from the copy, marked with the date it was copied. A missing phone is looked for in our own copies of the public registries first (the NPI Registry for health care, Recreation.gov for recreation areas), matched by name and street, and only then on the web; a listing our copies or its website flag as possibly closed is first in line for the Google check. If our main mail server doesn't answer, the site's own emails (sign-in codes, notices, receipts) go through our backup mail service, Amazon SES, from send.traversence.com. When the weather service can't give us a forecast, a place's guide shows a link to the National Weather Service's own forecast for it instead. If the check for a place's events fails, it's tried again the next day; otherwise each place is checked weekly. Something wrong? Use **Closed, moved or listed twice? Tell us** on the listing, or claim it if it's yours.

### What does "Possibly closed" on a listing mean?
The listing now says **Still open?**, with the source's own words. One source we read, often the business's own website or an open dataset, says it may have closed. It's a caution, never a closing: the listing stays up, with the source's own words. Under it you can answer **Yes, it's open** or **It's closed** (counted once a day per device, never who). When the notice has stood for 30 days without enough visitors saying it's open, or a visitor and a source both say it closed, our team checks: a call, a visit or a photo of its sign. Only our team or the owner marks a listing Closed, and a closed listing stays findable with **Reopened? Tell us**.

### How do you keep a business from being listed twice?
Every way a listing is made or shown again (our crawler's finds, the businesses' own "Add a business", our team's adds, a "Help us find this business" answer, a listing put back) goes through one identity check first. It compares the business with everything we list nearby, including listings that are hidden, by its name (with "LLC", "Inc" and plurals set aside), its phone (an extension doesn't count) and its street address written any way ("Rd" or "Road", a suite or none). If it's the same business, what's new fills the gaps in the listing we have, and nothing is listed twice. A separate check, switched on by our team, looks over the listings already live: two that are exactly the same business become one by themselves, two that differ on a detail (a suite, a second phone, a different website) are checked against public sources first, and only what the evidence can't settle, or a listing a business has claimed, comes to our team. Both stay on the site meanwhile. Another location of a business, or a doctor at a clinic, is never treated as a duplicate.

### Do you use open business datasets?
Coming, and switched off until our team turns it on. Open datasets that anyone may use (Overture Maps and Foursquare's open places, under their licences, credited) will tell our crawler which businesses are in a town we don't cover well yet. Each one goes through the same identity check as everything else, so a business we already list only gets what's missing filled in, and a find is listed only once it has a real name and a phone or street address we're allowed to keep, at the confidence its sources earn. A dataset saying a place has closed only asks our team to check it; it never takes a listing down by itself.

### Can search engines find Traversence?
Yes, the public pages: listings, places, towns, guides and the like. We publish a sitemap so search engines find them, and ask them (in our robots.txt) to leave out sign-in, your account, your messages, the admin and our own tools. A business that's private or confidential isn't in the sitemap. Staging, where our team tests changes, is never indexed.

### Why did a listing I saw move to another link?
It was the same business as another listing, so the two became one. The old link takes you to the one we kept, with everything we knew about both. If we got it wrong, tell us with **Closed, moved or listed twice? Tell us** on the listing: our team can undo it exactly, and the two are never put together again.

### Will you merge my claimed listing with another one?
Never without asking you. When a listing you manage may be the same business as another, our team asks you first, and what you entered is never changed by it.

### What is the number in the circle on a listing?
How sure we are of the listing, out of 100. It's on its card in the directory, guides, the map and search; on the listing's own page it's said in words ("How sure we are: Qualified", with **How we know**). Tap it to see how we know: each source that agrees (our own team, the owner, local people, an official registry, a government page, its own website, a chamber of commerce) and when we last saw it. Many rural businesses have no website or online record, so what our team enters and checks, the owner's claim and local people's confirmations count the most; having no website never counts against a listing. Red (up to 25) means our crawler found it and nothing else has confirmed it yet; orange (26 to 50) means some sources agree; gold (51 to 75) means enough independent sources agree; green (76 and up) means a person stands behind it: our team checked it, or the owner claimed it (with ✓ once they've shown it's theirs). Sources and computers alone never make a listing green, but they still count once it is: a claimed listing that other sources back up climbs toward 100. Older evidence counts for less, and a website that stops working or a closure report lowers it. Under the evidence, **Is this right?** lets you say Yes (it counts once a day from your device, and we never record who) or No (it opens a correction our team reviews).


### What's on a listing's page?
Its photo, then a card with, in its top-right corner, the **Link** button with **⋯** beside it. **⋯** opens what you can do: **Call**, **Email**, **Directions**, **Map**, **Website**, **Contact**, **Message** (or **Leave a message** when the business isn't on Traversence yet), **Save contact** and **QR code**; tap one, or ×, or anywhere outside to close it. The card leads with its name, address, town and phone, then its categories in small words (each one searches for more like it near there, with the most specific first and any "Other" last), who runs it or whether it's claimed, and **How sure we are**, which opens **How we know**. **Link** links you with the business (sign in first). **About** shows when there's something to say about it.
### Why did Traversence call my business?
To check your listing is right. Many local businesses have no website or online record, so our team calls the ones we're least sure of to confirm you're open and your name, phone and address are right. It takes a minute, and it's how your listing earns its green "Confirmed by Traversence" mark. We may also tell you how to claim your listing (free), so you can keep it up to date yourself. If we reached a wrong number, or you've moved or closed, tell us on the call, or use **Closed, moved or listed twice? Tell us** on the listing.

### My business is listed and I didn't ask for it. Can I change it or have it removed?
Yes. Claim it (**Claim it** on the listing) to correct it, add what's missing, or make details private. Or use **Closed, moved or listed twice? Tell us** on the listing to ask us to remove it, and our team takes it off the site.

### Someone told you about a business. How does it get listed?
If it isn't yours, use **Not your business? Tell us about it instead** on the same page. Our team checks it, adds it (a website isn't needed) and emails you when it's on the site. If you said it's yours, that email has a **Claim it** link.

### My business is listed, but I never added it. How did it get here?
Most listings come from public records and our crawler. Our team also adds businesses they come across: from a business card someone hands them, or from a receipt, a menu or a sign where they've been a customer. Those start unclaimed, with what was on the card or receipt, and our crawler fills in the rest. Claim it to manage it yourself, or tap **Suggest an edit** on it if something's wrong.

### How do I claim my business?
Open your listing and tap **Claim it**, at the top right of its photo (on a park or a public office it reads **Staff? Claim it**; on a nonprofit or a church, **Run it? Claim it**). You'll need a Traversence account (18 or older) with your email confirmed. Then:
1. **Confirm by email.** We send a code to your account email. Enter it and your listing shows **Managed by owner**: you can edit its description, hours, services and photos right away.
2. **Get verified.** Tap **Send verification request**. Our team gets your listing and how to reach you, checks it (usually the same day) and confirms it, and your listing shows **✓ Verified**. If they need something first, such as a call or a photo of the storefront, they'll email you, and you can send a new request when it's ready. You can also text your claim's code (it looks like TV-1234) to our Verify number instead.
3. **Choose a plan.** Free, or a paid plan (see below). Agencies and nonprofits stay on Free: plans for them are coming.

Your business dashboard (**Manage** on your listing) shows where your claim stands and what's left.

### Who can claim a park, a public office, a nonprofit or a church?
The people who run it. Each listing knows what kind of place it is, and its claim page asks the right things.
- **A park or a public office** is claimed by the agency's staff: a site or park manager, agency staff, or whoever handles its public information. The page shows which agency runs it (for example, State of Arizona › Arizona State Parks & Trails). If that's wrong, or it doesn't know yet, you can say which agency it is and our team checks it. The quickest way to be confirmed is a code sent to your email at the agency's own website. A claim covers the place you look after; claiming for the whole agency, every place it runs, in one go is coming.
- **A nonprofit or a church** is claimed by its director, pastor or leader, its staff, a board member or a volunteer.
- **A place on a tribal nation's land** that the nation runs: the nation decides who speaks for it. Until the nation's own tools are ready, your request comes to our team, who check with the nation first.

Agencies and nonprofits keep everything a claimed listing gets on Free; plans made for them are coming.

### What does "Managed by owner" mean?
Someone has added or claimed the listing and confirmed it by email, and it's waiting to be verified. Until it's verified it isn't shown to search engines. They can edit the description, hours, services and photos. Changes to the name, phone, address and website wait for our team until the claim is confirmed, so customers are never sent to the wrong place.

### I texted from a different number. What happens?
That's fine: in a lot of small businesses the owner's own cell is the business phone. Someone from our team will contact you at that number within 48 hours to confirm who you are and your role with the business. If you can't text, you can instead use an email at your business's own website domain (when your listing has a website), send a document such as a business license or utility bill (only our team sees it, and it's deleted as soon as your claim is decided), or ask for a call or a visit at a time that suits you.

### Why did Traversence contact my business?
Your business is listed on Traversence from public information, and claiming it is free. Someone from Traversence may
call, stop by, or email the address your business publishes, to offer you the listing: once it's yours you can
correct the details, add your hours and photos, and answer people who message you. We use only your business's
public phone, website and email, and never sell them. If you'd rather not hear from us, say so (or reply "no thanks"
to the email) and we won't contact you about it again.

### Someone else already claimed my business. What can I do?
Ask to claim it anyway. Only one claim goes ahead at a time, so yours is saved as **Claim Request On Hold**, not turned down, and our team is told straight away. Leave a way to reach you and anything that shows the business is yours. If the listing has a confirmed owner and you believe that's wrong, you can question it from the claim page: the owner is told and can answer, and our team decides. Nothing changes on the listing while that happens.

### I have more than one location. Do I claim each one?
When you claim one, we look for your other locations (the same name, phone or website) and offer to claim them together. Each location stays its own listing, with its own page. One confirmation can cover them all when it shows every location: your website lists them, or you send photos or a brochure that does. Otherwise each location is confirmed on its own.

### What do the plans cost?
For a business: **Free** costs nothing; **Basic Partner** is $29.95 a month, **Core Partner** $79.95, **Strategic Partner** $229.95 and **Cornerstone** $399.95. For yourself: **Personal** is free, **Personal Plus** $4.95 a month and **Personal Pro** $9.95. Paying by the year costs 10 months' price for 12 (two months free). Each plan includes storage and AI Assist credits, and each business plan adds tools to the one below it (Basic Partner adds customers, quotes and invoices, order emails and discount codes; Core Partner card checkout, a storefront and dynamic QR codes; Strategic Partner campaigns, wholesale price lists, your own workflows, the API and White Glove; Cornerstone several locations and the Co-Op hub). The **Plans** page lists everything under each plan, and **Settings & team** in your business shows what yours opens. No plan ranks you higher in search: plans pay for tools, storage and support.

### How do I choose or change my plan?
Open **Plans**: **Manage plan** in your business dashboard, or **Plans** in your Dashboard's menu, under Settings (also in the site menu, ☰). Pick a plan, monthly or yearly, and add your card. The card goes straight to QuickBooks Payments (Intuit); we never see or keep the number. A bigger plan starts right away and you pay the difference for the rest of the period; a smaller one starts at your next renewal, and you keep what you paid for until then.

### How does a free trial work?
When you claim your business and choose a paid plan, you first see a review: the plan, its price after the trial, the day the trial starts and ends, and what happens then. Tick that you agree to the Terms and choose **Start my free trial**. It lasts 30 days (our team sometimes offers 60 or 90), needs no card, and nothing is charged during it. We email you 5 days and 1 day before it ends, and on the day. To keep the plan, set up billing on the **Plans** page (add a card and choose **Keep my plan**): the price is charged from the day the trial ends, and you can cancel any time. If you don't, your business goes back to the Free plan on its own: nothing is charged, your listing and content stay, and the paid tools pause until you choose a plan again.

### Does my plan renew on its own?
Yes, on the same date each month or year, charged to the card on file, with a receipt by email each time. A yearly plan gets a reminder 7 days before it renews. Choose **Stop at the end of this period** on the Plans page any time: you keep everything until then and aren't charged again. Periods already started aren't refunded.

### What happens if my card is declined?
We email you and try again 3 days later, and once more after 7 days. Update the card on the Plans page and we charge it right away. If it's still declined after that, the plan goes back to Free; your listing stays up, and you can choose a plan again any time.

### What are storage and AI packs?
Extra storage (+25 GB, +50 GB, +100 GB, +250 GB, +1 TB or +5 TB) and +50 AI Assist credits a month, the same for every plan and for personal accounts. Add one on the Plans page once your plan is paid: you pay for the rest of the current period now, then it renews with your plan on the same date. Packs are always full price, also on a yearly plan. **Remove at renewal** takes one off at the next renewal.


### How do I use a promo code?
Type it where it says **Promo code**: on the **Plans** page, in your **GO Codes cart**, or on an order we've prepared for you, before you approve it. A trial code starts the free trial right away, with no card. A code for a percentage off shows what it takes off before you pay. If a code came with a QR code (a GO Code), scan it: its page shows the offer and takes you to where it works, with the code ready. Each code works once per account, between its dates, and some are only for one business.

### Why didn't my code take anything off?
A code works on certain things: plans, GO Codes, or kinds of work such as creative or printing. It never takes anything off storage or AI Assist packs. Some prices are already as low as they go, so a code can't take them lower. If you think it should apply, write to support@traversence.com with the code.
## Journeys and contributors

### What is a journey?
A story about a place you've been, in your words, with your photos. You can add a video link, tag the place and link the businesses you visited. Journeys appear on the place pages, so other people find them while exploring.

### How do I start writing journeys?
Become a contributor: go to your **Dashboard**, choose **Your journeys**, and click **Become a contributor**. Writing, editing and managing your journeys all happen in your Dashboard.
- You'll read the contributor guidelines and accept them.
- You'll need to be 18 or older.
- Your profile can't be private, because journeys show your name.

### How does my journey get onto the front page?
Publish it. Drafts are seen only by you and our team. A journey needs three things to show:
- **Published.** Open it from **Your journeys**, click **Edit**, then **Publish**.
- **A story of a few sentences** (at least 40 characters).
- **A place**, under **About a place**. Without one, it shows only on the North America page. With one, it also shows on that town, area or region.

Once published, it appears in the **Journeys & guides** feed right away, newest first.

### Where do my published journeys show?
- On **Let's Explore**, in the **Journeys & guides** feed for the place you chose, and the places above it, up to North America.
- On your **public profile**, under **Journeys**.
- On the **listing page** of every business you linked in your journey, under "Journeys that mention…".

A journey you post as a business also shows on that business's listing page.

### How does a photo end up in the big picture at the top of a page?
The picture at the top is chosen from published journeys at that place. Photos need to be at least 900 pixels wide, and the uploads we store are 2000, so yours qualify. The ones people like and comment on most get shown first.

### How do I get into Top Contributors?
Publish journeys as a contributor and keep your profile public. The list favors recent journeys people engage with, and accounts that have been around a while.

### My journey said "Saved as a draft: an automatic check found…". What now?
An automatic check spotted something it holds back: unsafe or spammy wording, or a pile of links. The message says what it found. Reword that part and publish again.

### The place I wrote about isn't in the place list. What do I pick?
The place list has regions, areas, towns and local names. For a lake, a park or a trailhead, pick the nearest town or the area. Mention the place itself in your title and story.

### What's the difference between "Traversence", "Contributor" and "Business"?
Every journey and guide is labelled:
- **Traversence:** written by our editorial team.
- **Contributor:** written by a member, with their name on it.
- **Business:** posted by a local business's team, shown under the business's name and linked to its listing.
- **Partnership:** the writer was paid, or given something free, for it.

### Can my business post journeys?
Yes. If you're on a listing's team as its Owner, Manager or General member, the journey editor has a **Post as** choice: yourself, or the business. A business journey shows the business's name and links to its listing. You still need to be a contributor yourself.

### Do I have to mark a partnership?
Yes. If you were paid, or given something free, for a journey, mark it **Partnership**. U.S. law requires the disclosure.

### Can I say exactly where and when it happened?
Yes, if you want to. **Where it happened** is an optional pin you set on a map, and you can add a time next to the "Was here" date. Each photo can also have its own pin and the date it was taken.

### My photo knows where and when it was taken. Does Traversence use that?
Only if you say so. When you add photos, your browser reads the photo's own date and location and shows them to you: "This photo says it was taken Aug 12 near …. Use it?" Tick what you want to keep. The photo we store has all of that removed either way.

### Where are my photos and files kept?
In secure file storage (Amazon S3, moving to Cloudflare R2), not on the web server: photos of places, businesses, journeys and profiles, a business's proofs and files, and order artwork. Photos you've made public load from there; private files (proofs, documents, order files, anything shared only with a customer) open through links that work for a few minutes and only for people allowed to see them. Everything you add is kept together in your own folder (a business's in its own), under a random name rather than your account number, so your photos' addresses don't say who you are. Removing a photo or file deletes it from storage, and deleting your account deletes your folder; files that belong to an order stay with the order, as its record. Documents sent to prove a claim are deleted as soon as the claim is decided. Downloading all your files in one go is coming.

### How big can a file be, and what happens when I upload one?
Every upload on Traversence works the same way, wherever you add it: the gallery, a business's proofs and files, an order's proofs, artwork and shipping labels, journey and profile photos, the photos at the top of a listing, a guest adding to an album, a claim document, a GO Code's logo. Before anything is sent we check the size: a file that's too big for where it's going is refused straight away with the limit, and anything over 10 MB asks you first, since it takes a while. Files go straight to secure storage in parts, with a bar showing how far along it is; if your connection drops, each part is tried again before it gives up. When it's done, the result shows right beside what you were working on (no jumping to the top of the page). Each file is checked to be what its name says (a PDF really a PDF) before it's kept. The most one file can be is **1 GB** (gallery videos and files, a business's proofs and files, order files). Some places take less, because they need less: a gallery photo up to 100 MB, photos for a listing, a profile or a journey up to 15 MB (we resize them anyway), a guest's photo 25 MB, a claim document 25 MB, a GO Code logo 2 MB. Photos are made a sensible size on your phone before they're sent, unless the album keeps originals (portfolio and brand albums).

### Can I leave the page while a big file uploads?
Yes. Once you start an upload, your browser keeps a copy of the file and carries on sending it while you move around the site: open another order, go to your dashboard, use other apps. A box in the bottom corner of every page shows each file with its size, how much has gone, one progress bar, and what you need to do before you go. On a computer, keep one Traversence page open (in any tab) until it reaches 100%. On a phone, keep the browser open and in front: phones pause uploads when you switch apps or lock the screen, and they carry on when you come back. If you close every Traversence page, the upload pauses and picks up where it stopped the next time you open the site in that browser (within a day). If the connection drops, it tries again every minute. When it's done, you get a notice with a link back to where you started it, and with **Tell me when it's done** your browser's own notification tells you even when you're in another app or tab. Closing the last Traversence page while an upload is still going asks you first. In a private window, or a browser that can't keep a copy, the page sends the file itself and asks before you leave it.

### Can I share an album with just a few people?
Yes. Open the album and choose **People**, then invite each person by email as **View only** or **Can contribute**. They get an email with a link; once they sign in (or make a free account) the album is in their gallery under **Shared with you**, whatever the album's setting. People who can contribute add their own photos (they stay theirs) and can take out only the ones they added; they can't remove anyone else's photos or change the album. You can change someone's role, take back an invitation, or **Remove** them: their photos come out of the album, and their likes and comments there are deleted. Anyone can also **Leave album**.

### Can my business have a gallery?
Yes. In your business's tools choose **Gallery** (or **Manage the gallery** on your listing page): everyone on your team with **Catalog & content** can upload photos and videos and make albums. The photos at the top of your listing are in it already. Public albums show on your listing page under **Albums**. A business's gallery counts toward its plan's storage.

### How do Portfolio albums work for my clients?
Make an album and, under **What it's for**, choose **Portfolio**. You set the rules: whether clients pick their favourites (and how many), downloads (none, picked photos only, or all, and how many each), and a watermark with your own text. Share it with its link or invite clients in **People**. Clients sign in, tap **Choose my picks**, then **Send my picks** with a note; you get an email, and **Client picks** shows each client's choices and lets you highlight them on the album. Clients see the watermark; you never do. Photos you upload to a Portfolio keep their full-size original, which is what a download gives (no watermark). Videos aren't watermarked.

### What is a Brand & documents album?
A place for a business's (or anyone's) logos, brand guides, PDFs, design files (Illustrator, Photoshop, InDesign, EPS, SVG), fonts, Office files, ZIPs, audio and video: any of them up to 1 GB. Choose **Brand & documents** under **What it's for**, then **Upload files**. Each file shows its type, size and version; **New version** uploads a replacement and keeps every older one, and the history icon lists them, each downloadable. If the album is shared, people who can see it can download its files. Searching and tagging files across albums is coming.

### Can Traversence staff see my private photos?
No. Our team's gallery tools list only what's already shared (public and unlisted albums, and what's in them); private albums and photos are only counted. If something shared breaks the rules, the team can make that album private or take a photo out of shared albums. It stays yours, in your gallery, nothing is deleted, and you get an email saying why.

### Can guests add photos to my album without an account?
Yes, for an event. In the album choose **People**, then **Let guests add photos**: you get a guest link and a QR code to print or show. Anyone who opens it can add photos (up to 60 a day each), with their name if they want; no account or app needed, and where the photos were taken is never sent. The photos are yours to keep or delete, and they show "Added by" the name they gave. **New guest link** or **Turn off** stops the old link and QR code at once.

### Can people like and comment on my photos?
Yes, anyone who can see a photo and is signed in. Open it full screen: the heart likes it (on a phone, double-tap the photo), and comments sit with that photo, not the whole album. You can remove any comment on your own photos, people can remove their own, and anyone can report one. Commenting needs an account that's 18 or older.

### Can I post a photo someone else took?
Yes, with their permission. Under the photo, choose **Used with permission** and say who took it. It shows as "Photo: their name".

### Can other people reuse my journey?
You choose, under **Rights and reuse**:
- **Shown on Traversence only** (the default);
- **Others may share it, with credit**;
- **Free to use, no credit needed**.

You keep ownership either way.

### Can I write a journey with someone else?
Yes. Under **Co-authors**, search for up to five members by name. They're invited when you save. Their name shows on the journey, and it shows on their profile, once they accept. Invitations wait on their **Your journeys** page.

### What does "Mature themes" do?
It puts a notice in front of your journey: readers choose to open it. Its card shows a notice instead of a photo, and it isn't used for the big picture at the top of pages. All Traversence accounts are for adults, but this lets readers decide.

### What's the "Caution" note for?
Anything readers should know first, such as rough roads, weather, closures or gate times. It shows as a banner at the top of your journey.

### Can I add a video?
Yes, as a YouTube or Vimeo link. We don't host video uploads.

### Who can see my comments?
Comments on journeys are public. You can remove your own comments any time.

### Can I edit or delete a journey?
Yes, from **Your journeys**. Deleting removes it and its photos.

### Who owns my journeys?
You do. Traversence shows them on the site with your name, and you can remove them any time.

## Photos and your location

### Does Traversence share where I am?
No. We never expose anyone's location. You may share your own, by tagging a place or a photo yourself.

### What happens to the hidden data in my photos?
When you upload a photo, we remove everything hidden in the file, including GPS coordinates, your device and the time it was taken. If you want to show where a photo was taken, tag it yourself with a place or a map pin.

### What's "Was here", and will there be "Am here"?
**Was here** marks a journey as a place you visited, with the month. **Am here**, live check-ins, is coming as a separate feature. You'll switch it on yourself after reading its rules and risks. Each time you check in, you'll choose to post now or later.

### Why do some photos say Stock photo?
Until a place, a business or a section has a photo of its own, a park, monument, visitor centre or historic site first gets a free Wikimedia Commons photo of the place itself, taken there and named for it (one taken right there, within about a mile, needs only the place's own name: "Lyman Lake" counts for Lyman Lake State Park), with its credit; the first time someone opens one still without it, we look for its photo first, within the hour; only when there's none do we show a **stock photo** instead: a photo nobody on Traversence took, from Wikimedia Commons, the Library of Congress or Pixabay, chosen by our team one at a time. Each one shows its credit on the photo ("Photo: <who took it> on <where it's from>") with a small **i**; tap it to see that it's a stock photo, whether it shows this place or only the kind of place (**Representative**), its licence and its source. We only use photos whose licences allow it, and never photos of sacred, burial or protected places. As soon as a real photo is added, it shows instead, by itself.

### What does "Representative photo" mean?
The stock photo shows the kind of place or thing, not this one itself: a desert road for a desert town, a shopfront for a shop. A business's stock photo is always marked Representative, because it never shows that business. When the photo really is of that place, the label says **Stock photo** alone.

### Where can stock photos be used?
Only where a photo illustrates: the photo at the top of a place's guide or of a listing with no photos yet, a card with no photo, a storefront's banner, the home page's sections and an event's backdrop. Never where a photo says "this is the real thing": a product or an item for sale, a journey, a story or a review, a gallery album, an event's own photos, a business's own photos (its premises, its people, its work, its logo), a profile picture, or a proof or an order. Those places don't offer stock photos at all, because authenticity matters.

### How do I add a photo or a file?
Wherever you add one (a journey, your profile, a Marketplace post, a catalog item, a listing's photos, a storefront), tap the button and your **Gallery** opens on the page, not your computer's file window:
- **Your gallery:** choose one of your photos (or several), sorted into catalogs. For the Marketplace, a catalog item or a storefront, the photo is used right where it is in your Gallery. A journey, your profile and a listing's photos keep a copy of their own, which then shows in that catalog too.
- **Stock:** only where a photo illustrates, like a storefront's cover (see below).
- **Upload new:** drag files in, paste a screenshot (Ctrl+V or ⌘V), or tap **Browse your device**, which opens your computer's or phone's own file window. What you upload is kept in your Gallery too, ready to use again.

It's the same for documents and order files (proofs, artwork, print-ready files, shipping labels, a quote's files): **Your gallery** shows the documents you've kept that fit, and a new file you upload is kept in your Gallery's **Documents** too, then added where you were. The order or quote keeps its own copy as part of its record.

### Does the Gallery know where my photos and files are used?
Yes. Each time you use a photo or a file from your Gallery (an order's proof, a catalog item, your storefront's cover, a journey…), the Gallery remembers. Open the photo to see **Used in …**, or look beside each file on the **Documents** tab. Only you (or your business's team) see this.

### Where are my documents?
In your **Gallery**, on the **Documents** tab: every file you've added anywhere on Traversence (proofs, artwork, quotes, brand files), newest first, with its size, its version and where it's been used. Search them, download one, or **Add documents**. To use one somewhere, tap that page's file button: it's under **Your gallery**.

### What are the Gallery's catalogs?
Your Gallery sorts every photo by where it belongs, so it's easy to find: **Uploaded here**, **Journeys**, **Profile** (or **Listing photos** for a business), **Marketplace** and **Storefront**. Documents have their own catalog and tab. **Orders** holds artwork from your orders with us that's been released to you; its photos go anywhere a photo does, your profile picture included, where you frame it like any other. Choose one from the **Catalog** button on **Photos & videos**, or from the list at the top of **Your gallery** when you're choosing a photo. Nothing needs filing by hand: a photo moves to its catalog as you use it. **Albums** are still yours to make, and **Stock photos** has a tab of its own, because they aren't yours.

### Where do I find stock photos?
In your **Gallery**, on its own tab, **Stock photos**, next to **Photos & videos** and **Albums**. Type what the photo should show and **Search**: you'll see what's already in the stock library, then free photos found at Wikimedia Commons, the Library of Congress and Pixabay, each with its credit and licence. Choosing one of those adds it to the library, labelled as a stock photo wherever it shows. One place for all your art, kept apart: your photos and albums are yours; stock photos aren't, so they never go in your photos or albums. Search them, see each one's credit, and, for a business, use one as its storefront's cover. When you pick a photo anywhere it's allowed, the picker has the same two tabs: **Your gallery** and **Stock**.

### How do I replace a stock photo with my own?
Add your own photo where it shows: on your listing, tap **Add your own photo** (or **Photos & video** at the top); on your storefront, upload a **Cover** in **Storefront & pages**. Yours shows straight away and the stock photo goes. For a place, share a journey with photos of it: journeys' photos show at the top of its guide.

### Can I choose a stock photo for my storefront?
Yes. In **Storefront & pages**, under **Logo and cover**, tap **Choose a photo** under the cover: your own photos are on the **Your gallery** tab, stock photos on the **Stock** tab. Or open your business's **Gallery** → **Stock photos** and tap **Use as storefront cover**. Visitors see it labelled as a stock photo until you upload your own cover, which takes over by itself. Your logo is always your own: stock photos can't be a logo.

## Posting guidelines and safety

### Can I post photos of other people?
Ask before you show people, and take extra care with children. You can mark a journey as showing people with their permission.

### What about private property and tribal lands?
- **Private property:** homes and private land need the owner's permission.
- **Tribal lands:** many nations restrict photography and filming. Some villages don't allow it at all, and commercial filming needs permits. Follow each nation's rules for visitors, and don't point others to sacred or restricted places.

Our guidelines are guidance, not legal advice. You're responsible for what you post.

### What can be reported?
- spam or scams;
- harassment or hate;
- sacred or restricted sites;
- someone's private information;
- people shown without consent;
- private property without permission;
- unsafe or illegal content.

### How do I report something?
Use **Report** on the journey, comment or post, choose a reason, and add a note if it helps. Our team reviews every report.

### What happens after a report?
Journeys publish as soon as they're posted, and our team responds to reports. If something breaks the rules, we hide it. A hidden journey stays visible to its writer.

## Suggestions and corrections

### Something's missing or wrong. How do I tell you?
Use **Make a suggestion** in the menu, or **Tell us** on a listing or place page. It works for anything: a business, a lake, a trail, a landmark, a story idea, or a correction to a listing. You can drop a pin on the map to show where it is.

### What happens to my suggestion?
Our team reviews it and checks it against public sources. Then:
- **A correction** (a phone, website, address, hours or name) is made on the listing.
- **Closed** keeps the listing on the site, marked **Closed** on its page and in search, so anyone looking for it knows before they go. Closed places come last in results and never show as open. If it reopens, tap **Tell us** on its page.
- **Listed twice** becomes one listing, and the other one's link takes you to it.
- **A missing business** becomes a new listing; **a missing lake, trail, park, campground or landmark** becomes a new place. Places whose names may mark a sacred, burial or protected site are held, not shown.
- **A missing event**: events are coming.

If you were signed in when you sent it, we email you when it's done. A business run by its owner is changed by its owner, so we check with them first.

## GO Codes and short links

### What are GO Codes?
GO Codes are Traversence's QR codes: for a link, a phone call, an email, Wi-Fi, a contact card, an event (it offers "Add to calendar"), text or a text message, in your colors and with your logo. Make one at [GO Codes](/qr/) (or just trvgo.us): choose what it does, type what it says, pick how it looks, and it's drawn as you type so you can scan it to check.

### What do GO Codes cost?
Each GO Code is **$4.95, once, plus sales tax**, and keeps working, unless its payment is refunded or charged back (then it stops working; see [What if something's wrong with my order?](#what-if-something-s-wrong-with-my-order)). With a free account you also get **5 free GO Codes** (link, phone, email or Wi-Fi in black and white), once your email is confirmed. Add codes to your **Cart**, then check out: the tax is worked out from your billing address, and you pay by card right there. Your card goes straight to QuickBooks Payments (Intuit); we never see your card number.

### What if something's wrong with my order?
All sales are final, but when something goes wrong that we didn't expect, in production or with files you approved, we make it right. Tell us at **support@traversence.com** within 14 days of receiving it, with your order number (and photos for printed items); if the carrier delays a package, the 14 days start when tracking shows it delivered. We **fix it first**: redo, reprint or replace what we got wrong. If it can't be fixed, you get a **full or partial credit** on your account, good for 90 days toward anything we sell. A **refund** is the last resort, when neither works.

What counts as a fix: for **digital work** (designs, video, GO Codes, plans), a code that won't scan or goes to the wrong place through our error, a file that won't open or comes in the wrong format, or something missing that was on the proof you approved. A change of mind after approval, a typo that was on the proof you initialled, or a new format or size isn't a fix; we're glad to quote it as new work. For **printed and made-to-order products**, damage in shipping, a printing or manufacturing defect (a severe color shift, bleeding text, cuts out of line with the proof), or the wrong size, material or quantity. Slight color differences between a screen and ink, and damage after delivery, aren't. **Custom and made-to-order products** are final once you approve the proof, so check names, numbers and spelling carefully. If a GO Code's payment is refunded, the code leaves your account and stops working; while a card dispute is open, it's paused. A refund goes back to the card or account you paid with, through QuickBooks, and we email you when it's made; your bank may take a few days to show it. The details are in our [Terms](/terms.php#fix-credit-refund).

### Do I need an account for GO Codes?
No. You can make GO Codes, check out as a guest, and download them. We keep them with your browser and email you a private link that opens them on any device. An account is recommended: your codes stay in one place, you get 5 free ones, and you can make Dynamic GO Codes. After a guest checkout we offer to open your account with the name and email you gave; your codes move into it, and so do earlier guest orders paid with that email once it's confirmed.

### Where are my GO Codes after I pay?
On **Manage GO Codes**. As soon as your card goes through, you land there with your codes ready, and your receipt email links back to them. (Paid on QuickBooks' own invoice page instead? Your codes appear there by themselves once the payment registers.)
